good morning colleagues. this parliament must continue to be multilingual. eleven languages make 110 combinations. twenty-two languages make 462 combinations. thirty-five languages make 1 090 combinations. can you imagine a translation from finnish, via english and french, to polish? with direct translation some will vote for budget line 2 while others are still voting on budget line 1. with more than one relay our system will not work. if we do not go for reform, then most of us will no longer be able to use our own language. in two years we might have ten new member states. our buildings are not prepared for that. soon even more countries – from the ukraine to the balkans – will be applicant countries. we risk a babel-like confusion. our next president must dedicate himself to internal reform and successful enlargement. indeed our next president will not have much time to travel around. we need a craftsman, a handyman, rather than a statesman. all of us will be to blame if we are not prepared for enlargement by 2004. how we vote today is part of that responsibility. every vote for me is a clear signal to mr cox and mr martin: limit your ambitions to enlargement and internal reform. today we lose a lot of time over badly prepared votes. if journalists ask how we voted, we do not always know. if we knew, we would not have time to serve our people. we would then be bad meps. leave it to the committees to prepare the votes. let the plenary deal with the important political issues. give us at least one week to consult and consider how we will vote. today many members are not allowed to speak in debates. if you represent a small delegation in a big group, or an alternative view, the group whips will seldom allow you to speak. give every member the right to speak at least three times a year. leave one-third of the speaking time for real debates. let us corner the commissioner when he or she states that the commission can accept amendment no 3 and rejects amendments nos 2, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 and 9. today common decision-making takes place between the commission and the council. they share the information. even in the conciliation committees our members have no access to working documents, legal notes or minutes. does "common" really mean "unilateral"? in the committees the members discuss draft laws based on outdated drafts. behind us sit young civil servants from the permanent representations. the commission and the council have all the information we cannot get. i have been here for 22 years. in that time as an elected member, i have never received adequate information from the committee. to get information i have to find sources like journalists. our next president must ensure that all information is available to all of us and, if necessary, take the commission and the council to court. today, 70% of all laws are passed by junior civil servants in working groups. fifteen percent are settled by the ambassadors. only 15% reach the level of the ministers who then read manuscripts prepared by civil servants. democracy was born in europe and buried in the commission and the council. our next president should re-establish democracy. he should inspire us to discuss whether our future europe should be the federalist vision of a democratic eu or a europe of democracies. our next president should unite all elected representatives from national parliaments and the european parliament in moving law-making from behind closed doors into the open, from civil servants to elected members. for what is now needed i offer my skills and energy. but all i ask is to borrow your vote in the first round: firstly, because it might be your only chance to vote for me; secondly, because many votes will test the groups behind mr cox and mr martin. this time we need a practical and politically-neutral shop steward to serve all of us. give yourself a better choice. improve on both cox and martin by voting mr bonde from office number 007. by the end of the day we will have a better presidency thanks to those who drafted the "fair chair platform" and created the first real electoral battle in our history. the next chair will have a real mandate. he deserves our full support.

- mr president, the smallest group in this parliament, the group for a europe of democracies, would also like to welcome the president of the council, mr aznar, and we would like to speak a little about the convention which will start on 1 march with 105 members, and which will be very skewed in composition, and with a bureau of 14 members, and which also looks to be very skewed in composition. i would urge you to ensure that among these 14 members there are also representatives of the 49% who voted no in the maastricht referendum in france, or the over 50% who voted no in a couple of referendums in denmark. it seems to me very inappropriate to put together a bureau of 14 like-minded representatives and produce a result which then after three or four years ends up being overturned by a referendum. it was more practical to enter into dialogue immediately with the different views of the people and then secure a lasting compromise. the intergroup here in parliament, called 'sos democracy', which is in a way the federalists' counter-poise and partner in transparency and subsidiarity, had an excellent meeting with mr verhofstadt and would like to request a similar meeting with you. during this meeting, mr verhofstadt made two interesting promises: firstly, he called for all delegations to include what we call 'eurorealists' from all countries, and secondly, for example, for at least one of the three representatives from two countries - two from parliament and one from the government - to be a person with a eurorealist attitude. he promised to work for this and urged us to campaign for it. the second was that he would support a subsequent referendum. my question to mr aznar is whether he too will call for a broad representation and ensure that referendums are held afterwards. will he help to ensure that two different drafts are drawn up, a constitution for a democratic federal state and a democratic agreement between independent countries, so that people can see the difference? will he be involved in holding an indicative referendum on two different proposals? will he be part of asking people what they think and then have an intergovernmental conference called to compromise? we must discuss how the charter can bind the institutions without getting into trouble with the constitutions and the court of human rights. how a division of the treaty into two parts does not just prevent the possibility of referendums on amendments. how more than thirty forms of decision-making can be reduced to three manageable forms. how to formulate a veto on vital issues without paralyzing the whole thing. how to involve national parliaments in cooperation and whether they can take over the commission's initiative, monopoly and competence catalogue. and how to make sure that it doesn't just go the way it did with the legality principle. and finally, in the context of transparency, which was also the subject of graham watson, i would ask you, mr aznar, whether you would change spain's position and ensure that parliament gets the same as the council of ministers. as a friend of spain, i would like to welcome you here and i hope that you will surprise and implement the openness that sweden did not achieve and that belgium did not work for. i'm with you.
- mr president, public procurement policy is a very important means of steering social development in a sustainable direction. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environmental impact of the use of energy is taken into account. it is a right, mr harbour, not a duty, although you have repeatedly tried to make it so. this is a right which the municipalities must have. this must of course be done with due regard to the fact that there will be no distortion of competition. the eu has some great environmental and social objectives, but if we do not integrate these objectives into all eu legislation, we will not get very far, and we will not get anywhere. the public sector must be able to impose environmental requirements on the products it buys, and it is particularly important to emphasise that it must be able to impose requirements on products throughout their life cycle. it is surprising that the eu's own eco-label has been given such a weak position in the commission's proposal, and when the public authorities draw up technical specifications, they must of course be able to refer to the eu's own eco-label. we have tried to include the social and environmental aspects in a number of amendments. i hope the commission will look positively at this in this instance.
- mr president, first of all, a thank you to commissioner wallström and to chris davis for a good job on this case. the ozone layer is a particular problem. the ozone layer is too thin in the upper atmosphere for the problems it causes, including health problems, and the concentration is too high down here on earth. but i think we have got a good result from the conciliation negotiations and probably a better result than many of us had dared to hope for when we listened to the negative attitude of some of the member states in regard to solving the air quality problem at issue. together with the directive on national emission ceilings, for which mrs riita myller, who is sitting next to me, was rapporteur, this directive makes an important contribution to ensuring a lower ozone concentration in europe and especially in europe's large cities. i think it is good that we have a starting point in the who's assessments of the existing health and environmental risks. we have set some sub-targets, some target values for 2010, which must be met. but it is of course sobering that for many years we have to accept that we operate with a certain excess of the who thresholds for a certain number of days a year. i therefore think it was good that we also agreed that there should be some longer-term objectives, in which the who's target values should be respected. it was not quite as accurate as we would have liked it to be in this parliament. a reference point was set for 2020. many of us would have liked to see 2020 become a more firm target for meeting the who recommendations. i also think it is good that the member states are not only obliged to draw up specific programmes for the objectives, but that there are also obligations to inform the citizens about the programmes being drawn up and the results achieved - both positive and negative results, just as we have rules for when the citizens must be informed of problems of excessive concentration of ozone - both citizens in general, but also citizens belonging to groups which are particularly sensitive to problems in this area. finally, i would say that i too think it is good that we have included the applicant countries so that they can be involved at an early stage, and we can now anticipate that the directive will enter into force before the first applicant countries join the union, so that they will also have to meet the obligations which the countries are bound by the directive at the time of their accession. i hope that this directive will help us to prevent and reduce the harmful effects on human health and on plant growth of excessively high concentrations of ozone and that today will be a good day for the future of air quality in europe.
advisory rapporteur to the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy. - mr president, it is now a year and a half since we were able to congratulate the commission on the cotonou agreement, and it is actually a bit embarrassing that only three of the eu countries have still ratified the agreement. i am well aware that there are probably some coming here during the spring, but still away it is not very satisfactory. it is at least good that we have our approval in place tomorrow, although i agree with the rapporteur that this is only the beginning, but i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on his report. admittedly, much has happened in the world since the agreement was signed in june 2000, but that does not make it any less important. i think it is fair to say that the fact that foreign policy is linked to development policy has been strongly emphasized since then, and i am also glad to be speaking today on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, because it shows the link between foreign policy and development policy. in a speech in berlin in december 2001 entitled 'from afghanistan to zimbabwe', commissioner nielson made these words his own. if one comes before the other, it might not matter. but 9/11 made this connection and the importance of foreign policy all too clear. september 11 showed what can happen if we do not ensure development in all parts of the world. it is now our task to follow up on these developments. the same is true of our follow-up in kosovo. it is also clear that foreign and development policy are interlinked in zimbabwe, but also that the eu has managed to reach an agreement that allows us to operate in zimbabwe with hopefully good results. i agree with the commissioner who in his closing remarks - and i should like to say that i agree with him - said in his speech that the c in the cfp '... stands for common, not convenient and colonial'. so we still have work to do, and i agree with that, and we still have work to do here in parliament.
- mr president, i shall concentrate on the eu's agreements with the developing countries. the report contains a number of good declarations of intent, but the reality is that they have never been put into practice, so the current fisheries agreements are neither an effective contribution to the development of poverty reduction nor an economic gain for the developing countries. one of the poorest countries in the world, mauritania, has just signed a fisheries agreement with the eu. experts have estimated that if the resources are managed sensibly, the octopus fishery alone could bring mauritania an income of $100 million a year, but mauritania cannot afford to develop its own fisheries and therefore receives far less compensation from the eu, which is over-fishing and is eradicating the octopus stock. in addition, 300 mauritanian fishermen lost their lives last year alone because of the increasingly dangerous fishing conditions. the eu takes from the poor and gives to the rich. these are the unfortunate facts.
- mr president, first of all i would like to thank mr lavarra for the excellent work he has done on this report. i can support the conclusions in general and i would particularly like to call for us to create new opportunities for development in the european fisheries sector by integrating environmental considerations into the common fisheries policy. the forthcoming revision of the common fisheries policy must also ensure a much greater implementation of the joint decisions in all member states. we must also dare to speak openly about the importance of fish as a raw material for the fishing industry in the countries with a large fishery, and here too weigh the risk and precautionary principle against regional significance. we must also acknowledge the great effort that fishermen make in their daily lives. i would like to make a few points of order. i would first like to thank mr stevenson for his very clear account of the experience of the committee on fisheries delegation in denmark. how could the mystery of the tobis fishery be taken out of denmark and thus also the many postulates that industrial fishing is what is destroying the food chain? we must therefore say that there is no scientific basis today for saying that industrial fishing has a negative impact on the marine environment. without a sound scientific basis, it is pointless to cut back on industrial fishing. this will have a very serious impact on jobs in the fisheries sector. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i cannot support paragraph 9, which is factually incorrect, because it would be extremely unreasonable not to use fishmeal and fish oil, which are very high-protein products and which are of very high value. moreover, the european fish-farming industry is very far advanced in the development of a technique capable of eliminating dioxin. in other words, in the short term it will actually be much healthier if we imagine that fishmeal is used to feed fish in aquaculture rather than - if i may say so - the normal food chain in the oceans, where the dioxin content is unfortunately higher than we would like. in this context it is important to note that dioxin does not come naturally from the sea and is not naturally present in fish, but is a contaminant that comes from land. unfortunately, there are too many food scandals, and it is therefore also important that we clarify that the fisheries sector is usually the victim, without it having been possible to prove later that it is this sector that is responsible for the scandal or incident in question. i should also like to say that the liberal group cannot support paragraph 14, since we are against financial aid for the construction of new vessels. i must of course - as mr stevenson said, and i was very pleased that he said it - call for support for the two amendments which i have tabled on behalf of the liberal group.
- in my view, both of these proposals contain a number of provisions which, in their own terms, are fair and humane. mrs ludford's report in particular is trying to live up to the sympathetic demands of fair treatment and active integration - that was just what was lacking! but i do have a few comments. firstly, we are now within fort europe, and the political attitude which dominates the european legislative space is defined by conditions of access so strict that the famous needle's eye almost looks like an open door. secondly, i must say that the proposals before us are a disgrace to the government of my country. our former liberal colleague, mr haarder, as the newly appointed minister for integration and expulsion, has pursued a policy which must have made his former colleagues wonder. the rules being negotiated today are considerably less hostile to foreigners than mr haarder's proposals. thirdly, the fact that the danish people have a xenophobic government which is overriding the eu rules by right-wing means that the people's movement, which i represent, will not change its views on schengen and title 4 of the eu treaty, from which denmark is exempt. this integration technique, used in the eu system of paragraphs, is deadly for the people's government. it is not the eu that is going to get rid of a government. it is not the eu that should implement a humane foreign policy, it is the danes themselves - we, the people.
- mr president, i should like to thank mr von wogau for the very well thought-out report he has produced on this difficult subject. it is a question which has been awaiting a solution for a very long time and i should be the first to welcome a solution. our debate today shows our general dilemma between wanting to be effective on the one hand and wanting to ensure democratic control of the legislation we adopt on the other. and it is this question of efficiency on the one hand and democratic control on the other that will be discussed in depth at the forthcoming convention. those of us who want clear rules of the game for the european institutions are often criticised for being navel-sharp and power-focused. i very, very much disagree. in denmark - my country - we had a constitutional struggle that lasted from 1866 until 1901, when parliamentarism was finally adopted as the guiding democratic principle. this is the same process we are now engaged in at european level. that is why i am very pleased that in the midst of this struggle we in the european union can deliver a result, namely this concrete agreement. with yet another reference to the situation in denmark 130 years ago, the entire political system was virtually paralysed during the danish constitutional struggle. i therefore welcome the statement by mr prodi that the commission accepts the content of this report, which should be strongly supported by the european parliament. but it is extremely important that the council accepts not only the content but also the basic message of this report. several members of the council think that this whole matter is basically useless and is simply a matter of a difficult parliament. that's not the case. in short, we are talking about democratic control of our common european legislation.

- madam president, as mr rapkay said, we can talk to each other as we usually do, now that we are gathered 'in the bosom of the family'. i would first like to thank mr monti again for his great efforts in this area, and then i would also like to thank mr rapkay very much. we do not always agree, but i am very pleased with the report, although there are some things we have to discuss as to how we are going to vote tomorrow. so there are a few specific things i would like to comment on. firstly, there is a matter which i have personally dealt with a great deal. that is the commission's proposal on the shipbuilding package. we had a debate here in parliament last autumn. i was very much against the proposed aid to the shipyards and therefore i would like to ask the commission how things are going with this case. i am glad to note that the council of ministers meeting in the council of industry at the beginning of december did not manage to reach the necessary majority to adopt the commission's proposal. the commission was not immediately willing to change its proposal, which was based on the findings of the so-called tbr report, which the commission produces every six months. naturally, i am concerned that the next tbr report from the commission will conveniently show that we need support for more types of vessels than those mentioned in the commission's original proposal. i would therefore ask commissioner monti to answer the following question: is there a new tbr report on the way from the commission, and if so, can the commissioner say what conclusions the report will draw? tomorrow we shall be voting in parliament on a proposal which we have not had time to discuss before the vote. it is true that this is a proposal which does not fall within your field of activity, mr monti, because it is a question of airlines being allowed, in an exceptional way, to maintain arrival and departure times at airports which they have not used sufficiently to be able to maintain under normal rules. we are therefore making special legislation, which we are now rushing through, which will of course mean that smaller companies who want to enter the market at competitive prices will not be allowed to offer this service to us consumers, who will instead have to deal with companies that do not supply the goods and - when they do - only supply them at excessively high prices. it is also state aid, mr monti, because it greatly favours the old national monopolies. i think this is an example of how our fine words about cutting state aid are too little followed up by concrete and firm action. i would therefore also like to ask you, mr monti, how this proposal relates to our general desire to cut state aid and create a level playing field for all of us.
the commission - madam president, i should like to begin by thanking the committee on economic and monetary affairs, and in particular mr rapkay, for presenting this excellent motion for a resolution. i am very pleased that this report provides the commission's support for a strict policy of state aid control. listening to this brief but interesting debate, i think i can see a broad consensus, as has been pointed out, between the various parties represented in this parliament. i also think that there is a non-ideological approach, and i believe that the more we develop the tools of knowledge, the more we can avoid thinking purely in terms of ideologies and thus be able to assess not only the quantitative aspects but also the qualitative aspects of state aid. i am glad to see that the committee is urging the member states to honour the commitment made at the stockholm council, to which mr kauppi also referred a short while ago, to reduce aid as a percentage of gdp. despite the positive developments in recent years, i too believe that there is still room for further reductions in aid in the member states. i agree in principle that the relationship between regional policy and competition policy needs to be carefully reviewed for the period after 2006. i also share the concern expressed in the motion for a resolution about the renationalisation of regional policy, since some of the economically stronger member states have said that a reduction in the spending of the structural funds should be offset by greater tolerance for national aid. i think that would be a very worrying trend. i am very pleased with the positive reaction of the committee and rapkay's report to two instruments which are perhaps not yet as user-friendly as we would like, and we will, for our part, always try to improve the state aid assessment framework and the state aid register which are now available on the internet. i do not need to describe what these two instruments are - you know them well - and the evaluation framework, in particular, already makes it possible to share useful information with the member states via internet links and, from the second edition onwards, also with the european parliament. in addition, the state aid register complements the evaluation framework with more detailed and less aggregated information on each aid case, indicating the sectors and undertakings concerned.

important, but changes in the way we work so we can deliver the results our voters want. and that brings me to my next point about contact with the populace. the precondition for contact with the really matters and where we have an impact. so the key here is reform, results and communication. and it's nice to hear you when you're communicating. you're excited, you're showing emotion, you're using colors, you're using the right words. i was just about to say that we actually have a female president because you use the feminine side of yourself, imagination and creativity. and that's what too. the convention is one of the most important documents for the future of europe. and it is important that we not only pill ourselves in the navel, but try to solve the tasks that lie ahead of us, and not lose ourselves in a dispute over whether it should be a 'constitution', a 'basic treaty' or a 'federation', but create a system where there is democratic control over political decisions. when we legislate for our citizens, the citizens must be able to see who is making the decisions and the people involved must be democratically accountable for those decisions. that is the message for the mr. president, your speech today demonstrates the value of an open campaign. we have taken the time to discuss our own situation and i would like to thank the chairman for his speech and discussion today and to put forward some suggestions for consideration. consolidation of laws could be achieved by refusing to consider or rejecting outright proposals which are not consolidated. it cannot be reasonable to have to collect 70 amendments to see the legal status of a fish. a transparency reform should ensure that all meetings and documents are open, unless a qualified majority adopts a reasoned exception, as we did in the martin/bourlanges report. political appointments should be a thing of the past. in future, everyone should be hired and promoted according to their qualifications, including here in parliament, and we could start over with the people who are now to be hired from the applicant countries. the plenary sessions must be brought to life. a third of the time could be devoted to spontaneous debate, as you suggested. the activists must be able to clinch a deal with the commissioner and the president of the council if they do not get an answer. all members shall be allowed to speak at least three times a year. we need to cut the votes so we can only vote on political differences. after each debate, the coordinators should meet and make the technical arrangements for the vote. the voting list should be known at least one week before the vote so that we can all consult our constituencies and know what we are voting for. the allocation of items should be revised. the d'hondt method could be changed to the saint laguë method, where you divide by 1.3,5 instead of 1,2,3,4,5. it gives the same proportional distribution, but it allows the smaller groups not to always have to take what the larger ones leave behind. and where the small groups are not represented, they can be given observer posts. the independent members should have their proportionate opportunities and we should ourselves remove all forms of discrimination rather than risk a new trial. the attack on small groups in the corbett report should be replaced by a gentleman's agreement where we achieve some of the objectives of corbett without cutting into the rights of the groups. in the convention, majorities and minorities should have equal access to qualified assistance and to make proposals and act on their behalf. there can be no special privileges for particular views. the europe movement and the federalists do not have the exclusive right to organise the debate with civil society at the expense of parliament. we must work together to make the whole of parliament and the convention work so that all members and all views feel that they are being treated properly and fairly. and today, at the meeting with mr giscard, we should like to ensure that all the alternates in the convention have full rights to take part in the work. good luck with the reform work!
- mr president, since the terrorist attacks in new york and washington on 11 september last year, we in the european parliament have discussed the threats to our freedom and democracy and our values in a more realistic light than before. most of us have come to realize that an attack on the united states is an attack on us. but even these few months later, there are circles which are busy relativising the threats, distancing europe from the united states. none of us likes the restrictions, the controls that affect us all and restrict the freedoms of everyone, but we have to do it because we do not know where and exactly who the enemy is. but we must face the fact that the divisions which divide the world economically and religiously and ideologically make it impossible for us to achieve the area of peace, security and justice that we would like to see, until these divisions are effectively removed. while i will vote for the framework decision on the fight against terrorism, i will not vote for the framework decision on the european arrest warrant because this arrest warrant was not created because of the threat of terrorism but serves a general federalist purpose and is not limited to targeting terrorists. i agree with baroness ludford that the level of legal certainty in all member states is not such as to justify extradition on the scale at issue here.
- mr president, mrs ferreira spoke beautifully about solidarity and the demands of the citizens to show solidarity with the third world countries. unfortunately, it is not the development we are currently seeing in denmark, where we are cutting back on development aid, which i think is a very unfortunate situation. of course, even if we have cut back, we still want to be among the largest donors, but in any case, we are sending the wrong signal from the danish side and the danish government at the moment. i also fear that the montreal conference will be just another conference without any concrete results, and that is why i would like to make three specific points in my speech here today. firstly, the need for development aid. i think it is absolutely crucial that we set concrete targets for when each eu country will reach 0.7%. we have heard of 0.7% since 1974, as i recall, and in the vast majority of countries it is still a long way off. and so i look forward to hearing what the commission has got out of its visit to the various countries with a view to very specific time frames. that's what it takes. the second thing i would like to encourage is that mr lamy's efforts to remove trade barriers, which were for the time being directed at the least developed countries, will also apply to other countries, because without trade and without the possibility of trade without customs barriers, it is not possible. the third point i would like to make is that we must look at what we can do from the public side to encourage private investment, and here i believe we can achieve a more fruitful cooperation than we have so far. so it is possible to take concrete action on the words of the world economic forum.
mr president, tomorrow parliament will adopt an excellent resolution tabled by several groups, in which article 8 mentions a number of countries, but of course there are countries not mentioned here, and i would like to urge them not to forget this meeting. i would particularly like to urge the council, when it speaks of combating torture, not to forget turkey, which still keeps political prisoners in total isolation. i would like to urge that we do not forget the prisoners of camp x-ray who are being held there in violation of the geneva convention. i would also like to ask that when we talk about political and social rights, we should not forget turkey, which is now in the process of banning the hadep party because it is approaching the 10% threshold. i would also like to urge that we do not ignore the fact that the united states has not yet ratified the rome statute and thus joined the international criminal court, and that when we talk about the death penalty we do not ignore the united states and china. and then i would say to the commission that it is very good that we are making these agreements which have human rights clauses attached to them when we make agreements with third countries. i just cannot understand why the agreements are not being respected more strictly, and i am thinking in particular of israel, which has an agreement with the eu that is clearly not being respected. i cannot understand why these breaches have no consequences, why the agreement is not terminated immediately.

continuing dialogue with the national parliaments, but also for how the committee can exert its influence in cooperation with the convention, because we really need all the good forces, whether they recent years, which is to build an international democracy that also has the support of the citizens. because, despite the fact that we have had this cooperation for 50 years, we must admit that we are only in the construction phase and that we may not have the full support and understanding of the project. we have experienced this many times in denmark, where it has been difficult to get the citizens to accept new treaties, and now we have also experienced it in ireland, and i just think that these referendums are symptomatic of what is happening in europe as a whole, and that is what we must take seriously. we may not take these two countries too seriously. it must be up to the national representatives to do so, but i think we must recognize that this is more than just an isolated danish/irish scepticism. it is not surprising that this is difficult, because we have been accustomed to using national parliaments as the basis of our democracy, and now we have to show that national democracies and international democracy can both exist and, above all, that they can work together. that's the challenge, and in a sense, i think we can say to ourselves that maybe we've been ahead of our time. if we did not have the eu as we know it today, we would have had to do something in response to the global challenge. in fact, it might have been easier if we had started from scratch, but now i think we should start from the starting point we have.
- mr president, there is a tendency whenever we have questions on women on the agenda, to have only women participating in the debate. i would therefore like to start by saying that there are actually two men from my group who have made an appeal for equality to parliament which will also be forwarded to the president. this 'enough is enough' appeal invites all groups to look at their own equality situation and take active initiatives to improve it. i would also like to thank the rapporteur for the excellent report which very well sets out an area where the situation of women needs to be addressed, and i will make a few individual points which i would like to highlight. firstly, i think it is extremely important that we have statistics and i would very much urge the commission to look at how we can improve the statistical base, because any discussion about women and men and inequality falls to the ground if we do not have the necessary statistical data. whether it's a surveillance agency or a database or whatever, it's less important. it is crucial that we have statistics. secondly, i think it is quite obvious, and therefore also relevant to this particular appeal, that the commission will ensure that women are involved in the negotiation of the association agreements. we know that very often we have only men at the negotiating table. in this case, the situation of women is reduced in advance and does not become a central theme. the third point i would like to make is the question of programmes. it is vital to examine what specific programmes we can offer to improve the education of women so that they too will be able to promote equality more effectively.

in writing. - what ? the report limits the role of national parliaments in exercising control over their respective national governments, a role which has been reduced since the extension of qualified majority voting in the council removed the link between decisions taken at european level and those taken by individual peoples. it also seeks to extend the powers of the european parliament, without recognising that democratic legitimacy is provided primarily by citizens at the level of national parliamentary institutions. the european parliament has been a key player in the process of democratization in the last few years, and it is the commission's task to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its powers in a democratic manner. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the nice european council indeed seems to have been aware of this fact when it included 'the role of national parliaments in the european architecture' (and not in the peripheries of this architecture) in the agenda of the next igc. in general, citizens of the european union regard elections to their national parliaments (and, in some countries, presidential elections) as a fundamental democratic choice between, for example, a left-wing or a right-wing government. the democratic legitimacy of elections to national parliaments is now being established and the institutions of the european union must therefore adapt to this. the council's most important decisions, however, must be ratified by the national parliaments.
in writing. - (da) sos-demokratie cannot vote for the napolitano report because it does not give the national parliaments a decisive role, whereas they are the only ones close to the citizens.
in writing. - (da) we decided to abstain from voting on the overall report. we believe that the commission's view that the directive has not been sufficiently transposed in denmark will eventually undermine the danish system of conventions. denmark had initially decided to implement the directive through collective agreements and leave it to the social partners to put things in place. however, since, as a result of pressure from the commission, the directive will be translated into danish law in the near future, we can only express our disapproval of the way in which the commission has interfered with a well-functioning system. however, we welcome the general improvements made by the report, in particular the clarification of certain concepts insofar as it has been shown that the formulation of several definitions has led to errors of interpretation in the implementation of the directive. many points in the text need to be clarified in order to safeguard workers' rights and improve safety and health at the workplace.
president of the commission. - we are dealing here with a problem of fundamental importance to which i must point out that we have not - all of us in europe - devoted enough attention. clearly we are all engaged in a battle in which most of us are very much in solidarity - liberalization of markets, unification of rules, elimination of interlocking, elimination of monopolies, elimination of privileges, operations which are certainly very important - but, and here i join the concern of the member who asked the question, we are also faced with a situation, in virtually all european countries, of increasing disparities. these are facts, statistics. i'm not condemning or judging. i believe that it is essential to renew the debate and to reflect on these issues, because the european model has always said something about the problem of justice, the problem of solidarity, and not just the problem of efficiency. there is no doubt that if we give up this, we are laying the foundations for the defeat of the european model. but we are giving examples of solidarity. enlargement is a unique event, the only one in the world where countries, realities with economic differences between one and four, are brought together. i wish everybody else in the world would try to do something similar to what we're trying to do. this is a tangible demonstration of solidarity. we must move forward in this direction, but i repeat that we cannot be satisfied with the distracted attention that we are all now giving to these great issues of solidarity and social balance. they deserve much deeper reflection in this period when perhaps excessive single-mindedness on these issues is prevailing.

president of the commission. i thank you, mrs randzio-plath, for returning to the problem of the mediterranean bank, because i understand that there are some other problems that need to be clarified. you have made a direct comparison with the eib, and it is precisely to avoid the bank's long gestation period that we have chosen a structure in which the european investment bank is in the majority. the eib has nine billion euros committed to the area, it has experts, it has a tradition. of course, i have had almost daily contact with president maystadt on this subject in recent days, to go into the technical aspects of the matter. they are trouble-free and ready for a speedy departure. i have to say that i am not in favour of the idea of a european parliament. why did i mention that, mr president? because, in my view, this shows that the eib is on its toes, it is absolutely prudent in pursuing this policy, but at the same time, unlike the facility, we can undertake operations which we have not undertaken so far: we can be much more active in the private sector, we can work with local, regional and national banks, so that we can multiply our action; so we can have a very different kind of activity. this structure also provides a partial answer to your second question. i see - and i repeat, we are still at the commission's proposal stage, so we will look into the matter - that the countries of the southern shore of the mediterranean seem happy with a structure in which they can link up, work together and participate in the shareholding and also in the board of directors. we have found this compromise formula so that there is active, responsible cooperation on the part of these countries - they participate in the boards of directors, they play an active role - but at the same time they can benefit from the experience of the eib, on which we can all rely. which countries will be taking part? it is open to the countries of the southern mediterranean, but we have not dealt with the particular problems because they will be the subject of a political debate with the council, nor the discussion which may also take place in the future with third countries. for now, i think it's only fair to start with this: europe - southern shore of the mediterranean; start working and see what the next opening you can get. i can assure you, however, that we also had problems of coordination with the world bank, which is already working in this field: the world bank, however, which has investments of about a quarter of the eib, spends four fifths of its resources in one country, turkey, so it has no general activity for the mediterranean, and the african bank operates throughout the mediterranean but on a totally negligible scale. the responsibility for the development of our bank will be absolutely vital, and the choice is precisely the balance between innovation and prudence.

- mr president, the bourgeois government of my home country denmark has today appointed an internationally recognized plasterer, a mr lomborg, as head of the government's so-called institute for environmental assessment, thus taking a major step towards abolishing all sustainable development. the eu presidency of the council overcomes the conflicts of society and the world more elegantly through verbal maneuvers, euphemisms and silences, but the result is basically the same. for example, the integrated transport system programme is claimed to be environmentally beneficial, but the development of the eu motorway network alone since the adoption of the maastricht treaty 10 years ago has resulted in a 15% increase in traffic-related co2 emissions, and this is completely independent of the policy decisions on reducing overall emissions. there is nothing in the programmes to suggest radical changes. the summit programme represents everything but sustainable development, and not just from the environmental perspective. what about the economic and social sustainability of this world? the main point of barcelona is to consolidate and expand the share of the eu countries in the economic world domination. it is not just the environment that is being sacrificed, it is the vast majority of the world's population. the link between the eu's realised capitalism and the eu's political and military oppression is frighteningly clear - only not to the eu elite.

the commission is not - mr president, in the 1999 discharge we have gone around the whole budget. all sectors have been illuminated. all the eu institutions have been read and signed, because although the commission is ultimately responsible for implementing the budget, it is not alone. it is bound by decisions taken in parliament and in the council and depends on the effectiveness of controls in the member states. the agricultural sector is a prime example. the commission proposes a reform of the agricultural policy, but the council and parliament object and propose that the systems be made needed. we do not always agree with the commission, but we have basically the same objective, namely that the commission should become an efficient and modern administration. the most important thing - mr president, the report contains a number of good intentions for the south caucasus, which is undeniably a volatile problem area. europe has an interest in resolving the conflicts in the region, of course, but i have to express my doubts about the ability of the eu to play such a major role in the region as this report proposes. i would therefore like to stress that although the possibility of eu membership is mentioned only in the long term, these countries do not naturally belong to the eu membership circle. all the many fine words are hardly enough to solve the major problems in this area, and i would venture to doubt the commission's capacity to bring about the desirable changes. i would like to add a few comments on amendment no 2 to paragraph 15. i think it is wise that mr duff proposes a formulation which speaks only of settling historical differences. i would therefore urge you to vote in favour of the amendment. events during the first world war, which neither the british nor the american governments could classify as genocide according to the investigations at the time, cannot be described with any positive benefit, as they are mentioned in paragraph 15 of the report. this is not the way to achieve the normalisation of economic and diplomatic relations. it is true that the land border between turkey and armenia is closed, but this is not a blockade in the usual sense of the word. there are air links between the countries and armenian citizens can enter turkey. the closure of rail and road links is a consequence of the other unresolved political and diplomatic situation. what the eu can do is to work towards a joint negotiated solution to the issues that separate the two countries. it takes thought and diplomacy. the european parliament is not promoting a solution by making us the judges of events in the distant past. the uen group will vote in favour of the report, subject to the adoption of amendment no 2 to paragraph 15.
in writing. - (sv) what is it? the lisbon decisions are not a coherent strategy, but two different strategies, which were included in the same text of the decision. the first is to promote cutting-edge technologies and economic innovation based on research. the other strategy aims at achieving full employment, social inclusion and lifelong learning for all. it would be commendable to try to achieve a social compromise, but this has not been achieved with the policies pursued, and it is now becoming increasingly clear that some governments and political parties in the member states want to go in a different direction. the report by mr bullmann is an attempt to maintain the policy of integration and compromise. the amendments adopted today in parliament mean, however, that parliament is now taking the side of the governments, who are proposing a clear change of course. although we acknowledge the rapporteur's efforts to defend serious social objectives and although we supported many of his proposals, we consider that the report as a whole follows a clearly right-wing approach and proposes a course which is going to create major problems for the ordinary citizens of europe. we are also concerned that the commission, the council and parliament have taken little account of the needs and interests of the applicant countries in the preparations for the barcelona summit. we therefore voted against the report in its new, amended form.
in writing. - (da) - what is it ? the european parliament's left group abstained from voting on the report on the situation in the south caucasus. the members of the left in the european parliament support amendment no 2 by mr andrew duff and others, which strongly calls on turkey and armenia to work together to reconcile their historical differences with a view to improving their cultural, economic and diplomatic bilateral relations.
the commission is not - mr president, the european union faces major challenges in the coming years, all of which require greater public commitment. the eu must therefore develop a new information and communication strategy for its citizens, in order to increase their awareness and understanding of what the eu really stands for and does. the aim of a new information and communication policy must be to look at information and communication from the citizen's point of view, and two things must be taken into account: the citizen does not generally distinguish between the institutions of the eu - the council, the commission and the parliament - for him it is all "brussels"; and the citizen wants his information from the sources closest to him, including the physical ones. the main messages of this report are therefore coordination and decentralization. coordination between the institutions of the eu's information and communication to and with citizens and the maximum possible decentralisation of all information and communication activities of the eu institutions. it is very welcome that the commission and parliament have resumed and strengthened their cooperation in the interinstitutional group on information, and when the group meets tomorrow we can be even more pleased that the council will be taking part for the first time in this important coordination of work. in the field of decentralisation, it is important that the member states participate more actively in the work. decentralisation means that the information, responsibility and resources required are allocated to the representatives of the member states, so that the tasks are carried out as closely as possible to the citizens and in the way that the citizens of the country, region or local community want. today, too much information about the eu is published in a general form, for example the same brochure translated into 11 languages. but the citizens of lapland and sicily do not necessarily share the same wishes as regards the content of information and the way in which it is received. coordination is understood as high-level overall planning, i.e. the level of the interinstitutional group on information. in the report, the european parliament asks the commission to draw up an annual report on all the activities - including those carried out in the respective directorates-general, because nobody seems to have a complete picture of what is happening here. this report deals with the information and communication framework. the commission will be making a further communication on the content in a few months' time. as regards the framework and the tools, we know that over 60% of the public want to receive their information on television. we must then consider whether we should create a european television channel dealing with european policy. the c-span model in the united states, the bbc parliament in the united kingdom and the german parliamentary channel phoenix, as well as the dk4 in denmark, are different models which could be considered at eu level. the commission and parliament have joined forces to create a service called 'europe direct' where citizens can get answers very quickly on the internet or by telephone to find out where to go to get the information they need. a service which has already been very well received by the citizens of europe - that is a fine initiative. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we must be very careful to avoid propaganda from the eu institutions. the information must be as reliable, impartial, accountable and pluralistic as possible, and in the case of pluralism, it would be possible to draw attention to views other than those of the institutions by linking the websites of the institutions to the websites of sceptics, ngos and others. the implementation of this report will cost money. we ask the commission how many there will be. on the other hand, the task is of the utmost importance for democracy, and today the budget for information in the eu is about a quarter of a euro per citizen per year. this is a small sum in relation to the task, especially in comparison with the sums spent in the member states on information from national and local authorities. i have many people to thank for the help they have given to this report, officials in the commission, in parliament and in the council, many experts and ngos in large numbers - but first of all, of course, my colleagues in the interinstitutional group on information and in the committee on culture, youth, education, the media and sport. thank you very much.

the commission is not - mr president, i would like to begin by making a point of order, and i assume that it will not be taken from my speaking time. i think we should try something new in parliament today. what i would like to do is to divide my five minutes so that i am allowed four minutes to speak as an introduction and one minute as an ending to the debate after the commissioner has spoken. i do so on two grounds: firstly, i understand that the conference of presidents had an away-day last week, when they decided to have some more lively debates in this chamber, and secondly, on the basis of rule 121 (4) of the rules of procedure. i asked the meeting service before i came here today if that was possible. i received a flat-out 'no', but i would ask for permission to do so on the grounds that there is nothing in the rules of procedure to prevent such a procedure. so i assume, mr president, that you will be in favour of doing it this way today.
the commission is not mr president, i would like to say again on the rules of procedure that my task after the debate is not to criticise anything the commission has said but rather to comment on some of the points made by my colleagues in this house. and now i'm going to start my speaking time! noise is a major problem in european workplaces and i think it is important to stress that this directive is good for employees and does not affect others. as has been discussed, there are many misunderstandings as to who is affected by this directive. we know that noise can have incredibly harmful effects. the effect of noise is well documented. we're talking about permanent hearing loss and tinnitus. i believe that the fight against noise at work is the best thing we can do to avoid workers being permanently excluded from the labour market and thus prevent occupational injuries. i am therefore very surprised that, during the consideration of this matter in the committee on employment and social affairs, the eldr group, for example, which usually attaches enormous importance to doing something for the disabled in the union, has not shown greater interest in taking preventive action. so avoid people being handicapped by hearing loss, because that is the best strategy. we also know that the existing 1996 noise directive is outdated and does not provide adequate protection for workers. the common position which we are now presented with is much better because it introduces for the first time a real limit value. the establishment of such a common minimum level for the whole of the eu is an important step. the amendments adopted by the committee improve the common position on a number of points and i welcome the support that several have said they will give. the greatest improvement can be achieved if we give priority to the preventive strategy, and i believe that this is a good and right principle. now to the music and entertainment industry. it is a very controversial subject and the pse group has reached a compromise with the eldr group and the ppe-de group on this, and we are of course in agreement. but i will not hide the fact that this is a deterioration of the common position, and it is interesting that we now see a european parliament which places less emphasis on the protection of workers than the council of ministers does. this is a new development and i ask the public to take note of it. we support the compromise proposal because it was important to us to avoid the common position being rejected in the european parliament. and that, as we heard, was a real risk. that would be clearly unacceptable, because then workers would only be protected at the 1996 level. that is why we have accepted the compromise, and it also shows, of course, that we have been extremely attentive to what the music and entertainment industry has told us on this matter. i would also like to say to my colleagues in the house that we may have been too responsive at times. i think that some in this parliament have been too uncritical of what has been said by the industry. these are the worst rumors ever heard about this case. i've been accused of trying to shut down the entire pop industry in britain. i have been accused of wanting to ban the bagpipes in scotland. of course, all of this is sheer nonsense! we must listen to what the surrounding community tells us, of course, but we must not turn our backs on it. it is a compromise proposal that i can live with. i'm actually quite happy with it. this will mean that the entertainment industry will not be covered by the directive for five years, and in the interim period we will instruct the commission to carry out a study of how the directive will affect the music and entertainment industry. the commission will then assess whether it is necessary to exempt the industry from the directive or to regulate it in some other way. i think this compromise proposal will make it clear how much exaggeration has been done in this case and also make it clear that even people in the music industry are very seriously affected by the hearing damage that we are actually trying to combat. in conclusion, i would like to stress that i would have liked to see us go further in improving the protection of employees. it has not been possible, but what is crucial now is that colleagues tomorrow support the proposals which actually improve the common position and actually make sure that we put more emphasis on the preventive aspect. i would urge all members to support the proposals now before the committee and the compromise proposal.
the commission. - mr president, i should first like to congratulate mrs thorning-schmidt, because she has really managed a very difficult technical and political dossier. the difficulty of this dossier is clear when we consider that the amended proposal has been pending in the council since 1994 and that, just now, the first part of the proposal, i.e. the amendments, is ready for discussion by the co-legislators. we have given priority to health and safety through the social agenda and we have undertaken to achieve better protection of workers' health and safety by aligning the existing directive 86/188 with framework directive 89/391 and updating certain of its provisions. i would like to point out that some of the proposals that have been put forward go beyond the existing framework and the specific existing directive. hearing loss is certainly the most well-known adverse effect of noise. it's the most serious, but it's not the only one. but as regards the social compensation of workers suffering from occupational diseases, we must note that one third at european level is related to hearing problems. in five countries where studies have been carried out, in the netherlands, portugal, spain, france and germany, these social compensations for hearing problems cost 96 billion euros a year. the commission accepted the council's common position as it increased the level of protection provided for in the commission proposal. the reduction of the exposure limit from 96 db to 87 db is a clear progress. the commission fully accepts amendments nos 1, 2, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 14, 17, 18, 20 and 22, which improve the quality of the text and clarify its meaning. the commission accepts in part amendments nos 4, 15, 16 and 19. the commission rejects amendments nos 3, 5, 13 and 21 and amendments nos 23 and 25. although i can agree with the ideas underlying amendment no 3, the second part of amendment no 4, amendment no 5, amendment no 13 and the second part of amendment no 16, for reasons of consistency and legislative technique the commission cannot accept them. the commission must also reject amendment no 21, because it considers that the way in which member states obtain access to relevant medical and scientific advice is a matter of subsidiarity. finally, i would like to refer to compromise amendments 23 and 25, which seek to temporarily exclude music and entertainment from the scope of the proposal. the commission strongly opposes this exception, as it is not justified on scientific grounds. the implementation of health and safety measures in the music market affects 3 to 5 million workers in europe. a comparison was made between factory noise and music or a concert. i think the numbers speak for themselves. a musician who is constantly in concert is exposed to a constant high level of db, which can be as high as 130. his constant exposure to this noise is gradually creating a problem with his hearing, which is the main element of his work. i would also like to remind you that hindrec, the european association of hotels and restaurants, agrees with the proposal currently on the table. and i would also like to give you the clue that in the small pubs the existing db is of the order of 75, so far below the limit of 90 that has already been set. finally, we must say that the earplugs available on the market and i would ask you to do a quick internet search, where you can see all these new tools and that they are not the classic earplugs, but what they actually do is to reduce to a small or large extent, depending on the choice of each worker, the sound to which he is exposed and thus, to be able to comfortably carry out his work, without at the same time suffering this gradual damage to his hearing. finally, i would like to remind you that there are two other physical factors which affect work, namely optical radiation and electromagnetic fields, and that work should begin on the next two directives, which are also very important and concern a large number of workers.

commission. – the commission wishes to thank the honourable member for her questions and her interest in the commission's work concerning the matter of takeover bids. it is still, however, a bit early for the commission to provide final answers to those questions. the commission has launched a thorough examination of the recommendations made by the group of high level company law experts relating to takeover bids and has consulted interested parties in order to decide whether and how such recommendations could be included in a new proposal for a directive. the commission is aware of the criticism raised by certain member states regarding the specific recommendation on multiple voting rights and the possible loss of certain rights after a takeover bid via the "breakthrough" mechanism. the commission will, wherever possible, do its utmost to take account of the various opinions expressed during this phase of informal consultation with all interested parties. i am afraid i can say no more at this moment, but no doubt mrs riis-jörgensen, the committee of which she is a member and myself will engage in dialogue as soon as a more concrete proposal has been formulated. i would also like to ask mrs riis-jörgensen to contain her impatience until then.

commission. – recent research at the virginia institute of marine science has found the brominated flame retardant penta-bromodiphenyl ether (penta-bde) in freshwater fish. previous studies have shown that penta-bde is also found in mothers' breast milk. that is the brominated flame retardant that belongs to a group of ten chemicals known as polybrominated diphenyl ethers, pdbes. three polybrominated diphenyl ether flame retardants are available on the market. these are: penta-bde, octa-bde and deca-bde. penta-bde is used almost exclusively as a flame retardant in polyurethane foams such as those found in vehicle upholstery and household furniture. at present there is a commission proposal to ban the use of penta-bde as from 1 july 2003. a major use of octa-bde is as a flame retardant in acrylonitrile butadiene styrene (abs) plastics, whereas deca-bde is the flame retardant of choice in high impact polystyrene. these plastics are used to mould, for example, housings for computers and as packaging for electronic appliances. due in part to the presence of brominated flame retardants, including octa-bde and deca-bde, in electrical and electronic equipment, the commission has proposed that member states set up separate collection schemes and ensure the proper treatment, recovery and disposal of such equipment. furthermore, in a parallel proposal the commission has called for a ban on the use of polybrominated biphenyls and polybrominated diphenyl ethers in electrical and electronic equipment as from 1 january 2008. the common position adopted by the council on 4 december 2001 brought forward this date to 1 january 2007. at present, octa-bde and deca-bde are undergoing risk assessment within the framework of council regulation 793/93 on the evaluation and control of the risks of existing substances. risk reduction measures in addition to those mentioned above may, if necessary, be proposed in the future.
- mr president, i thank you for your reply and for the attention paid to this matter. but i remember back then, when you could not find a substitute for asbestos; what really started it was that you wanted to ban the substance. i understand now that you want to make collections, that's fine too. but it is not just a question of what happens to the material when it is used up, but also what happens to the material while it is in use. that in fact, when used, it releases substances that we absorb partly in our blood, partly in our fat layer and partly in our breast milk. we ask whether, now that there has been a study suggesting that brominated flame retardants are present in breast milk in 40 times the amount previously thought, it was not a good idea to make a long-term plan for when it can be banned also in the last two editions, which i shall not try to pronounce.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation - mr president, first of all i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs isler béguin, on her excellent report. my only objection is that, since the majority of the world's biodiversity is found in tropical areas of the developing countries, i would have liked to see the development aspect given a little more prominence in the report. paragraph 49 requires the eu to support measures aimed at encouraging indigenous peoples to manage and conserve biodiversity. i have tabled an amendment which changes the wording to support the role of the eu in the conservation and management of biodiversity, which is already played by indigenous peoples through their traditional knowledge. this is particularly true of protected areas, where it is important that indigenous peoples participate in the management of the area in order to avoid forced displacement and lack of access to resources. i have tabled an amendment to include this aspect in the report. in some developing countries, there is a clear need to be able to reap economic benefits from the sustainable exploitation of wildlife if the population is to have an incentive to conserve stocks. in southern africa, elephant populations have become so large that they are destroying biodiversity, and in the economic situation of the countries, it is completely unreasonable that they cannot make a living from their elephants. biodiversity is being lost today at an unprecedented rate, yet nothing has really happened since rio. biodiversity has become a concept that is being put on the back burner, and it is now our task to try to keep it on the agenda. to tackle the issue of conservation, it is necessary to protect the entire ecosystem. the community's cooperation programmes have proved to be viable alternatives to a protectionist approach, and i can fully support this way of thinking.
- mr president, mr commissioner, i should like to begin by thanking my good colleague, marit paulsen, for her great, skilful and competent work on this report. i support most of the content of the report and am pleased that the commission is prepared to propose transitional arrangements for waste water treatment. but in some areas i think we are going too far. at the last part-session we discussed karl erik olsson's report on the implementation of the bse decisions in the member states. that was sad. there wasn't much to look forward to. there are too many countries that are sick and this leaves too many gaps in the system for an effective fight against bse. we owe it to ourselves and, above all, to the countries which are complying with the decisions to do something about it. i cannot support the proposed amendments to the post-mortem blood approval rules, for which i do not think there is any professional justification. and i also lack some estimates of the economic impact of this in the member states and the regions, as i fear that it will impose heavy costs on small businesses, and especially on the very small butcher.
