- mr president, as a dane, i naturally hope that this can be done here, so that we can have a 'from copenhagen to copenhagen' and be able to put the final signatures in copenhagen in the autumn. but as a democrat and an internationalist, i have my doubts whether we will get there. i think there are still many obstacles, some of which have been mentioned, but i think there are others which are also worth mentioning. we heard about the development from the fall of the wall to today. the countries of eastern and central europe, which have emerged from a planned economy and militarisation, were mentioned. yes, thank you, but what are they getting at if not planned economy and militarization. the other day we had a debate in the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy on transatlantic relations, and all the panellists felt that european countries should aim to raise their defence budgets to between three and five per cent of gdp just to live up to the general expectations of the eu's military capability. what does this mean for the applicant countries and their economies? i fear that the people of eastern and central europe, when they realise what conditions we are offering them, will vote no in the final referendum, and that would be a disaster in my view. so i am not satisfied with the economic package that has been made in connection with enlargement. i am not satisfied with the idea that it should be free. i am not satisfied that the applicant countries have to go through a transitional phase of 10 years before they have the experience of full participation in the economic sphere, when at the same time we are the ones who set the transitional conditions. we are the ones who do not want to apply the free movement of labour to the applicant countries on an equal footing with others. when such things come up for the people who will have to vote in due course on whether the applicant countries should become members or not, i fear we will have problems. i would therefore urge that in the final phase extra efforts be made to change this by saying that it must not cost anything. i fully agree with what mrs schröeder of the verts/ale group said, we are talking about probably the biggest peace project of our lifetime. do you really want to go on about how it's free? are we really so petty in this cooperation that we think that what is the greatest peace project of our lifetime should cost nothing? i would very much like to help to campaign for the rich member states to pay for this, and then that will also apply to my own country. denmark must be willing to pay - of course. to say that it must not cost anything is to say that we are not really going to go ahead with this huge project wholeheartedly.
- mr president, i would like to warn against treating the new applicant countries as second-class members. the convention brought together the national parliaments and appointed their two representatives to the bureau, without the representatives of the applicant countries being invited to attend. i submitted a proposal on 25 february that the applicant countries should then be able to elect two representatives to the bureau. the proposal was neither distributed nor considered. the bureau held its first meetings without representation from the new countries. they must of course be fully equal and also have the right to be interpreted into their own languages. this weekend the prime ministers are meeting in barcelona. the big countries are attacking the principle of equality inherent in the rotating presidencies. there is a risk of a situation in which the eu is governed intergovernmentally by representatives of the large countries, who then act collectively supranationally vis-à-vis the smaller countries. that's how i read solana's summit speech. it is a variant which the critics of the union should not be the only ones to criticize. the proposals are also a signal to the applicant countries that they can join but not as equals. the commission's approach to agriculture also operates with two classes of members. it would be wiser and cheaper to abolish price support in the eu before enlargement and to compensate the applicant countries and our own farmers. the signal about the different treatment is wrong and deeply harmful. it is also the decision of the nice summit to give the czech republic and hungary two fewer seats in the european parliament than the eu countries with fewer inhabitants have. i would once again urge the commission to publish the so-called screening reports. they are about how far the applicant countries are from adopting our legislation. this is information that belongs to the citizens - both in our country and in the applicant countries. here, all citizens are discriminated against. the negotiations on legislation should be conducted in full openness, as in all parliaments, and the citizens of the applicant countries must also be able to follow the development of their own laws. solana's new briefing should have been presented directly to us, as i requested, instead of me now hearing about it through leaks. let us put an end to this secrecy and bring the commission to justice if it does not hand over the screening reports.
- mr president, i too would like to thank the council and the commission for their work and i hope that despite the political 'hurdles' which will probably arise, it will still be possible to conclude the negotiations in copenhagen. but then we come to the next stage, namely, the public's support for the project through referendums. and in this connection i would like to mention one aspect which covers half the population - namely women. there is no doubt about it, and the commissioners have also assured us that the eu's acquis communautaire will be implemented. but one is a formality, another is a reality, and the reality is that the situation of women in the applicant countries has not improved since the fall of the wall. i would therefore ask whether the commission and the council are doing anything to take account of the possibilities for strengthening the position of women in the countries concerned. if it does not, we may have a problem with the referenda, we may have a problem with the representation of women in parliamentary elections, and we may have a problem with the representation of women in public administration. so i would ask the commission and the council to say whether any specific initiatives are being taken for the women in the countries concerned?
- mr president, the overall message of this debate is that membership of the european union is something that is geared towards the maximum political happiness of the member states and of the union as a whole. i wish that assumption were correct, but i am unfortunately convinced that the message of the union project will prove to be a gigantic and terrifying illusion. wishful thinking blocks out any sober realism. several problems have been mentioned, but they are not the decisive ones. there are the politically inflamed problems of the bonn decrees, there is the cyprus question, and there is the economy with the unresolved problems of agriculture and the structural funds and so on - and the budgetary problems and the fear that the member states, the applicant countries, will become second-class members. but the fundamental problems are not political or economic. the crucial problems are that the process in question in relation to the eastern applicant countries involves a huge transformation. it is a question of providing the legal framework for a process of transformation of historical dimensions. and behind these legal problems, which can be difficult enough, are hidden democratic problems. the final copenhagen criterion is the need to provide a public administration that effectively and correctly administers, applies and implements community legislation. there is no information in the existing reports on this gigantic problem. and even if you get an effective legal system that is able to carry out the transformation, it will be democratically compromising, because how will you involve the people in this process? the moment of truth is approaching.
commission - mr president, the commission fully accepts amendments nos 1, 2, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 14, 17, 18, 20, 22 on this report. we believe that these amendments improve the quality of the text or clarify its meaning. we accept, in part, amendments nos 4, 15, 16 and 19 and reject amendments nos 3, 5, 13, 21 and 23 and 25.
the commission is not - mr president, this is a point of order. yesterday when we debated this question, i asked the president whether we could have a different way of conducting the debate and perhaps have a more lively debate by speaking for four minutes and then rounding off the debate with one minute of my speaking time. the chairman was very kind and said that we could do that, and everything went quite smoothly. my question to you, mr president, is whether you will instruct the bureau that this is indeed a procedure that can be used here in parliament and that it is supported by the college of presidents in order to create a more lively debate. when i asked the meeting service whether that was possible, they actually said no to the possibility of the debate being conducted in that way. so i would ask you, mr president, to instruct the secretariat that this is the procedure we use in this house. it creates more lively debates and there is nothing in the rules of procedure to prevent this approach from being used.
council. - mr president, on 6 february 2002 the commission presented to the council a proposal for a regulation extending the provisions of regulation (eec) no 1408/71 to third-country nationals who, solely on the grounds of their nationality, are not covered by them. the proposal is based on article 63 (4) of the ec treaty. the council has agreed on the possibility of using article 63 (4) as a legal basis, as proposed by the commission. article 63 (4) itself requires the council to adopt measures defining the conditions under which third-country nationals legally resident in a member state may reside in other member states. the council has considered that article 42 of the ec treaty, interpreted in the light of the case law of the court of justice - in particular the judgment in jalil c-95/99 - does not appear to constitute a sufficient legal basis for such an extension. mr president, it is clear from the jalil judgment that the court did not object to the inclusion of stateless persons and refugees in regulation (eec) no 1408/71, based on article 51 - now article 42 - of the ec treaty, only in so far as it concerns a small category of persons in respect of whom the member states have undertaken international commitments. the court held that the council could not be criticised for having taken into account stateless persons and refugees residing in the territory of the member states in the exercise of the powers conferred on it by article 51 of the ec treaty, in order to respect the international commitments of the member states which it had referred to above. as the advocate general pointed out in point 59 of his opinion, 'coordination which excluded stateless persons and refugees would have led the member states, in order to ensure compliance with international obligations, to have to establish a second coordination system intended solely for this very small category of persons'. the council therefore considers that the procedure for adopting a decision is that provided for in the ec treaty. if the legal basis adopted for extending coordination to third-country nationals turns out to be article 63 (4), the council shall act in accordance with the procedure laid down in article 67. as regards frontier workers, the council has reached agreement in principle on sickness and unemployment benefits, according to which the situation of former frontier workers in retirement should be examined. under the provisions of the present regulation, a frontier worker may receive sickness benefits in kind in the territory of the competent state, but no longer does so when he is a pensioner. for reasons of continuity, in order to continue treatment initiated, a former frontier worker should be allowed to continue to receive sickness benefits in kind in the territory of the former state where he worked. finally, as regards the unemployed person who, during his last period of employment, resided in the territory of a member state other than the competent state, the main objective is to integrate him as well as possible into the labour markets. in addition, it will be appropriate to ensure the social protection of the unemployed taking into account the financial aspects of the matter and to ensure effective control. as regards the relationship between taxation and social security, this is essentially a matter for the member states and not for the union. it should be noted that, in general, the 2002 employment guidelines draw attention to the need to adapt tax and benefit structures in such a way that they do not have a negative impact on participation in working life, particularly for women. nationals of the candidate countries who become community nationals by the mere fact of accession shall, by virtue of the accession, be included in the scope of regulation (eec) no 1408/71. the possible exceptions to this principle, which could take the form of transitional periods, for example, will be determined in the framework of the accession agreements currently under negotiation.
the commission is aware of the fact that the council has not yet adopted the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. it is not necessary to repeat its textual reading, which the presidency has already done. we believe that the jalil judgment of october last year raises the inadequacy of the legal basis of article 51 (currently article 42) and calls for a review of the legal basis of the decision proposed in 1997. since the entry into force of the treaty of amsterdam, with the new provisions of title iv, we think that the appropriate legal basis is article 63 (4) of the treaty, bearing in mind at the same time that article 308 of the treaty can only be applied if there is no other legal basis. the honourable member also asks the commission's opinion on a number of parameters raised by the council, namely parameters 6, 10 and 11, relating to agreements between countries, unemployment benefits and family benefits. as regards the first point, the commission considers that parameter 6 is acceptable, as this possibility is already provided for in the current regulation. the commission is pleased that this regulation, unlike the current one, sets out criteria for maintaining such agreements, especially in the most favourable cases for the person concerned. secondly, as regards unemployment benefits, the commission regrets the lack of progress on parameter 10, which does not bring about any substantial change in the current provisions, particularly as it does not offer any concrete solution to the situation of frontier workers. as regards family benefits, parameter 11 does not exactly correspond to the commission's proposal, but the commission is in any case pleased that it provides for the maximum amount of benefits to be guaranteed to the beneficiary. this parameter will improve the situation of pensioners and orphans compared to the regulation, since these persons will benefit from the same provisions applicable to the same category of family benefits as employed and selfemployed persons. the relationship between taxation and social security is another of his questions. it is true that regulation (eec) no 1408/71 concerns the coordination of social security schemes in the member states. there are no explicit rules for the coordination of these tax systems and, for the most part, this issue is resolved by the bilateral double taxation agreements concluded by almost all member states. these agreements follow the oecd model, which means that in most cases income from paid employment (and also from selfemployment) is subject only to taxation either in the country where the activity is carried out or in the country of residence. if they are subject to taxation in the member state in which the activity is carried on, the same country is responsible for both taxation and social security. the court of justice confirmed that, in the absence of harmonisation at community level, the member states are competent to establish the criteria for allocating fiscal competence in order to eliminate double taxation, but in doing so they are logically bound to respect the principles of community law and, inter alia, the prohibition of discrimination on grounds of nationality. i have made my final remarks on enlargement. firstly, as the presidency has said, the negotiations with the applicant countries on regulation (eec) no 1408/71, in the part concerning chapter 2, have been provisionally concluded - or have been concluded - with all the countries except estonia and, within this chapter 2, the coordination of social security schemes is also included. however, negotiations on the annexes are still pending. the administrative commission for the social security of migrant workers has been instructed to present shortly to the council working parties the results of its discussions on the entries which the applicant countries have requested to be included in these annexes. finally, regulation (eec) no 1481/71 will apply to nationals of the candidate countries as soon as they join the european union, since no transitional period is provided for in the field of the coordination of social security schemes.
- mr president, i agree with the commission and the council's interpretation of the treaties, of course article 63 must be used as the legal basis for guaranteeing the free movement of thirdcountry nationals. and i have also argued in the past that the legal basis may not be the most important thing at the moment, because it is about ensuring the rights of these citizens. but i think that in the long term we must also get parliament to have a direct say in this area. that will require an amendment of the treaties, and i am ashamed to say that my own country - because of the danish reservations - fought against our having influence on these matters during the negotiations for the treaty of nice. and i quite agree with mrs weiler that we must move away from the situation where some countries have reservations about parts of such a policy. we all have to pull on the same hammer.
- mr president, i shall deal primarily with the proposal on supplementary supervision of financial conglomerates, which i have dealt with a great deal in my capacity as shadow rapporteur of the eldr group. first of all, i should like to thank the rapporteur, mr lipietz, for the enormous amount of work he has done in the course of parliament's consideration of this proposal for a directive. secondly, i would like to thank mr radwan and mrs villiers, with whom i have had very close contact during our work on the vote tomorrow. last but not least, i would also like to thank commissioner bolkestein for his work in creating a genuine internal market for financial services. on the vast majority of points, we in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, with the active participation of the commission, have found common solutions to the problems raised by different groups. the background to this specific proposal is that we want to ensure stable financial markets in europe. we need to avoid scandals like enron. but at the same time, we must not regulate more than is necessary. the key issue is therefore to ensure effective controls on the use of the same capital by financial conglomerates to consolidate in several different locations. i would like to express my support for the oral amendments which mr radwan will table to amendments nos 44, 46 and 48 in connection with the vote tomorrow. i understand that the commission will also be able to accept the amendments tabled. i think that the adoption of the resolution tomorrow is further proof that the european parliament is taking part in the responsibility when necessary. i am a strong believer in not dragging things out if possible solutions emerge. we have found those solutions here in parliament, and it is only up to the council to provide the rest.
- mr president, i too would like to begin by congratulating mrs carlotti on her report. it is a fine job that has been done here and it is also very good that the work is being completed before the next meeting of the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly, which is already next week. it is an important report and mrs carlotti has made a number of points of principle. i will go into some of them, but before i do i want to stress that it is not very helpful to stand here and talk about the cotonou agreement if we cannot get it ratified, and we must once again call on the member states to make this possible. i think that in the last few months only one additional country has come on board. and although many have planned ratification, we have not yet seen the final signatures. the situation is somewhat better with the acp countries, but the total number is by no means large enough yet. the second thing to say is that such an agreement is not worth very much either, if we do not get proper aid. it is also a difficult one, and i hope that the commissioner - or whoever he has as his assistant - will succeed in getting a process started before the formal foreign ministers meeting tomorrow. it is certainly not the 0.7% target i would have liked, but i must admit that it is impossible to achieve at the present time. but the very fact that a binding process is being set in motion will be absolutely crucial. i would like to take up two points in mrs carlotti's report. one is the question of our own working methods. i do not think we have gone far enough in that respect. i think we have too many decisions and not enough conversation. the second point is the composition of the acp-eu delegation, which is not very appropriate. the fact that there is only one person from the acp countries creates a bias, democratically speaking, and that is why i also think we should look at it again. for example, should we have twice as many from both sides and then only meet once a year to get a larger representation - or what else can we do?
- mr president, the summit has now set up a working party to carry out reforms of the council's work, and this is badly needed. today, 70% of all laws in the eu are adopted by officials in the council's working groups, 15% are adopted by the musketeers of the ambassadors in coreper, 15% reach the council of ministers. i do not have a percentage of what the ministers see, but i do know that the legislative power belongs to the electors and the elected representatives, but that in the european union it has been seized by officials and their successive ministers. we could replace our own ministers, but we can never vote for new laws in elections. if a law is not working well, it can only be changed if we get the commission to put forward proposals and get it adopted, usually by 62 votes out of 87, behind closed doors in the council. the european parliament can, by an absolute majority, propose amendments and reject a law under the conciliation procedure, but the european parliament has not been given the legislative power that the electors and our elected representatives in the member states have lost. we are still facing a growing democratic deficit. i would suggest that all eu legislation be dealt with in parallel by the national parliaments. that's where people can follow; that's where democracy starts. the next reform is to open up the council's deliberations to the public. the negotiations on the laws should be public, as in the national parliaments and the european parliament. the votes should also be publicly available. meps should have access to all documents in council working groups and why not also look at the ombudsman's proposals for administrative reform. during the negotiations on the regulation on access to documents, germany, france and spain were the three main opponents of greater openness. now aznar lópez has the chance to give spain a different image. it could be the june summit in seville, which will go down in history as the place where the eu really opened up to its citizens. i also hope that it will be the place where the attempts of the big countries to take over the presidency of the european union are killed. we should not have group presidencies where each of the five large countries has four or five small states in a group presidency - then there is the risk that the big country will take the lion's share. small countries must also be able to represent the common foreign and security policy in the external world. we must appear as a cooperation of independent countries, not as a new state. if the big countries want to act together, they must unite in a common state. they must not be able to use the common institutions to marginalise the smaller member states.
the committee on budgets has adopted a number of amendments. - madam president, on behalf of the committee on budgets, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr carlos coelho, and the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs and justice for having incorporated most of the amendments of the committee on budgets into the report. the proposal put forward by the commission brings together five existing programmes - grotius ii, oisin ii, stop ii, hippocrates and falcone - and i think that the proposal is broadly in line with parliament's wish to ensure more support for projects which can provide the citizens of the union with a union of greater security. internal security is one of the priorities which parliament also highlights in the guidelines for the budgetary procedure for 2003 - the year to come - which were adopted at the last part-session here in strasbourg. one of the aims of the programme is to ensure that police and judicial cooperation will be more closely linked in the future, and the committee on budgets regards this as an objective with the not insignificant effect of improving the use of financial resources. it is welcome that the applicant countries will also be able to participate in the projects financed under the programme, but it must be clear that parliament, as the budgetary authority, cannot accept that the financial perspectives be exceeded, so the programme should be limited to the period 2003-2006 in the first instance, and thus be shortened by one year. this of course means a corresponding reduction in the budget. then you can extend it. finally, i think it is important that the budgetary authority be consulted if it should become necessary to increase the appropriations for the programme. the increase in appropriations for this programme must not have the effect of reducing appropriations for other programmes which are also financed under category 3 of the financial perspective.
- madam president, in the grand political picture this report is a trifle. in economic terms, this amounts to less than one per cent of the annual eu budget, and the immediate political consequences are neither to be applauded nor disputed. this is a programme under title 6 of the treaty on european union. it is clear that as a lawyer and a democrat i must say that cooperation and development are always good and that they can be useful and enriching, but it is very difficult to take the rhetoric seriously. the reasoning for the report reads as follows: 'if we want to ensure that european citizens enjoy an area of freedom, security and justice, we must ensure that criminal investigations, prosecutions and executions can be carried out fully across borders.' it is as if freedom, security and justice were invented with these resolutions, but the fact is that the vast majority of the judicial, criminal and police decision-making is done at national and local level. this is a rhetorical trick which contributes to or should be seen as part of a trend towards the unification of police, judicial and criminal policy decisions. as far as the economy is concerned, we can say that eur 65 million is also a kind of money, and it is another item in this long series of programmes which are entering the eu's administrative procedure. i shall not go into detail, but i will simply point out the peculiarity of the commission's statement that these programmes have been evaluated. but they were evaluated in 2000, even though the vast majority of programmes were not adopted until 2001. it's an impressive amount of clairvoyance, which promises to be good for the projects.

the commission is not - madam president, i would like to recommend that we give the commission a discharge for the development fund and i would ask mrs schreyer to convey this to mr nielson, as i see that he is not present today for this important debate. i have in the meantime threatened to postpone the discharge decision if the commission could not deliver some results. it has. there are still some problems, but we must look at them in a follow-up report. i have focused particularly on quality in terms of the number of follow-up audits this year. what concerns me is that the commission does not have an overview of how many audits have been carried out, by whom and where. one of my first questions to the commission was: 'how many audits did you carry out in 2000?' the first response was 12 audits, and i think that was very, very few, considering that there are 70 acp countries. then the commission found some more reports. they thought that there might have been some reports made in the delegations and nielson assured the committee that 73 audits had been made in 2000. i then asked for a list of these audits. it turned out to be a list of very sparse information, so it didn't make much sense to me. but it was a great laugh - especially in the court of auditors - when they saw what was being presented as audits. almost half of them were from malawi, and it turned out that they had to be deducted, so we were down to 52 audits. and not, as nielson promised us in committee, 73 reviews. it may be that 52 audits are enough, but it is worrying that the commission does not have a better overview of how many audits are being made and who is doing them. there is no central archive and no overview of activities "in the field". however, the commission has convinced me that it is not necessary to carry out the audits from the headquarters in brussels. that is why i have withdrawn my original request for at least 50 audits. but the more financial responsibility is placed on the delegations, the greater the demands on the headquarters' oversight and coordination. i have also asked the commission to give me a better explanation of why the remaining millions of euros mentioned in the court of auditors' annual report have not yet been recovered. it is two years since this was discovered and nothing has happened. i asked the director-general of dg-aidco if, on the basis of the control system and the information received from the delegations, he could give a statement that edf funds had been used in accordance with the principles of 'good administration'. i haven't heard back yet. i could have put the question to commissioner nielson if he had been here today. but i think mrs. schreyer might be able to help me. there has been much criticism of the way in which the acp secretariat has operated. audit reports have been made which are very, very critical, and unfortunately there have also been cases in the belgian courts where people have been convicted for not complying with the law. i do not think we can live with that in the eu. there is a new management now, and i have had some very good meetings with them. i hope that things will get better, and that is why i have said that we will now wait and see what the outcome of the court of auditors' decisions will be, and then we will have to take up the matter again. i would also like to make a few comments on behalf of the gue group on the mccartin report. i think that is a good result and i would like to commend him for having listened to my many amendments and for having included them. it is one of the ways in which we can follow up on previous discharge decisions, and that is very, very important. however, there are two important matters that i am not aware of: when will the disciplinary proceedings in the stockholm office be closed, also by olaf? and the same applies to the irela case in spain, which we have been talking about for so long. i should also like to say a few words about the discharge decision for parliament. virrankoski has done an excellent job. it's sharp and it's balanced. it is not easy to understand that we are asking for transparency in connection with the discharge decision for parliament. everyone here is on the up and up, especially when it comes to their own money. i would like to say that i am deeply disappointed that the ppe-de group's secretary-general has written to all the other groups that it would be a disaster if there were any transparency about our accounts. we criticize the commission for not being open, and when it comes to ourselves, we close our eyes. i really think that the ppe group's secretary-general should be deeply ashamed of calling around and asking for total secrecy about taxpayers' money. we can't live with that. i would also like to say that one of the things that has come to my attention is that the financial supervisory authority is to be abolished. if you look at the figures for 2000, you can see that 7% of the documents were wrong. i think it shows that the internal audit that is now coming must be able to be independent. but i still say, do not close down the financial supervisory authority. as regards the discharge decision for the council, i would like to say that we support the committee's comments and decision. i cannot accept that some members of a group are being beaten into a position where we have to change the decision we voted for in the committee on budgetary control. but on the whole i would recommend that we give discharge in all areas. it's going well, it's going much better, and we can only be happy about that.
- mr president, i should like to begin by congratulating my colleagues on the committee on budgetary control and its chairman, and also thanking the staff of the committee and the groups who have done a very large and thorough job. this is the great work that has led the majority to recommend that discharge be given. i cannot go into detail on these many matters - nor can others - because of the limited time available, but i would refer to the excellent comments made by my colleagues virrankoski, blak and morgan. i shall concentrate on mr mccartin's report on the discharge to the commission. i would like to quote two points: in paragraph l it says: '... which refers to the fact that the financial year 2000 was marked by a significant increase in the level of fraud and irregularities detected by the member states and olaf'. point p reads: 'recalls that the decisive factor in the assessment of the implementation of the budget for the financial year 2000 is, firstly, the community's administrative structure, which should provide the basis for effective management but is deficient, and, secondly, the parts of the system which are conducive to fraud and irregularities.' and so it goes on with a series of sharp remarks which, when read, give the impression that we are dealing with a south american banana republic. the european union was not created a few years ago, this system has been in place since 1957, and it is completely unsatisfactory that we - year after year - write these reports with clear and strong points of criticism, but nothing gets better. for next year we will write something that is confusingly similar to what we are writing this year, and then we will still discharge. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the financial management and the legislation itself are unacceptably bad. in my country, a government with such an administration would be overthrown and the officials responsible fired. we cannot be aware that so much of the money of the european taxpayers is being lost in fraud and irregularities. the citizens simply do not want to accept this, this cannot be the price of cooperation in europe. the only logical conclusion is, of course, to vote against giving discharge to the commission.

in writing. - (da) i voted today in favour of the european parliament's report on an action programme on administrative cooperation in the fields of external borders, visas, asylum and immigration. i would, however, draw your attention to the fact that the proposal concerns an area covered by title iv of the treaty establishing the european community which does not apply to denmark (see protocol on the position of denmark).
in writing. - (da) i voted today in favour of the european parliament's report on the examination of an asylum application. i would, however, draw your attention to the fact that the proposal concerns an area covered by title iv of the treaty establishing the european community which does not apply to denmark (see protocol on the position of denmark).
mr. president, nations have the means of power, police, currency, military. the european union's desire to have these means of power is not justified by practical reasons. cross-border crime could be fought through interpol. instead, europol is to be the starting point for the european fbi. the core of monetary union is not a single currency for international trade, but the prohibition of national currencies, which will lead to a single economic government. and the purpose of the rapid reaction force is not just to ensure peace, because that can be done through the un and the osce. the hidden agenda is the desire for a genuine common military, which could eventually replace nato. the eu must be a state with a common police force, currency and army. my group has a different vision of europe. we want a europe of democracies, where independent countries cooperate on everything we cannot control individually, but we do not want the eu to become a new superpower with a superpower military. the eu is the world's most powerful trading bloc. we have great powers with a history of colonial power. it is not certain that people in other parts of the world will look upon us as peacefully as we do. for the sake of peace, too, it is better to be satisfied with the un, the osce and nato rather than building a new military power.
- mr president, there is nothing to indicate that sharon intends to follow any of the recommendations coming from either the eu or the united states. nor is there any indication that israel is currently concerned about its reputation. israel, on the other hand, respects money, and therefore the only way to put pressure on israel is to demand preliminary discussions on our trade agreements. i was in israel in january, and there were huge demonstrations against cuts in aid to students and to the elderly. of course, like any politician, mr sharon wants to be re-elected, and that aim would be thwarted if his people were subjected to economic sanctions because of his government. the eu has been trying for too long to influence the sharon government with condescension, and it has only meant that sharon has denied the eu access to speak with arafat. all my friends in israel, who i met when i landed in 1967 on the first volunteer plane and then stayed there for two years, agree that sharon's state terrorism is destroying israeli society from within, and that is why we must also stop sharon for israel's sake. when will the eu say enough is enough? and when is the european union going to call what sharon, his soldiers and settlers are doing, namely killing thousands of civilians and innocents - men, women and children - what it is: terrorism. because before we put that label on sharon's actions, he can continue to cut off supplies of food and water and medical care by shooting at ambulances, all in the confident belief that we are not going to do anything but talk and that we will never act.
- mr president, in the debate on palestine yesterday, there were many references to chechnya, and there are also a great many parallels between the situation in the two areas. like palestine, chechnya is a country occupied by a strong and great power. as in palestine, the people of chechnya are experiencing the violation of their very basic, fundamental rights, and we are even experiencing in chechnya a country that has been completely reduced to ruins, where 20% of the population has been killed. it is quite clear that both israel and russia are taking advantage of the fact that the united states has declared war on terror to wage war against an entire people under the cover of this war on terror. a war that is approaching ethnic cleansing. i think we should remember that there was no terror in chechnya when, in 1991, it took advantage of the law of 16 december 1966 that states within the federation also had the right to independence, to become independent of russia. there was no terrorism then, but since 1994 russia has done everything it could to prevent this independence. it's another example of how the oppression of a people, how the desperation of a population, can lead people to do things they otherwise would never do. terrorism doesn't just happen by itself. terrorists don't fall from the sky. it is not that certain populations have special genes that make them behave differently from the rest of us. it's the conditions people live under that make them behave the way they do. it is important that we take a decision to invite maskhadov and putin to strasbourg in september. so we can have a debate, so we can have a statement, so we can impress both sides. i think we must demand open access for humanitarian organizations and the media so that the war of information does not play into the chechens' hands. but finally, it is also necessary that people who have been subjected to abuse have a clear sense that those who violate human rights will eventually be punished. without such an experience, i do not think we will get very far in the fight for democracy and human rights or in the fight against terrorism. we must demand an end to the russian occupation of chechnya, and we must demand it today.
- mr president, i would also like to thank the rapporteur for a good and balanced report which reflects the increased common interests that exist between the eu and china today. china is becoming an increasingly important player on the world stage in all respects, and this also applies to the fight against terrorism, where we must appreciate that china is actively supporting the serious fight against terrorism. the eldr group wants a democratic china which respects the principles of a free market, human rights and the rule of law. we want china to meet the copenhagen criteria too, there is no reason for these criteria to be limited and kept within the limits of europe. the eldr group has tabled a number of amendments, all of which are intended to tighten up the report a bit. the most important issue for us in this context is the 'one china' policy. we all know about it, and we all know why it exists. as liberals, we are not proud of the 'one china' policy, because it prevents the development of a country which respects democracy, social pluralism and the rule of law - namely taiwan. the eldr group believes that taiwan's wishes and will must be respected and that china policy is standing in the way of this. we also believe that the european parliament is the right place to send this signal from, so it must be up to our governments to follow our advice. last year, taiwan's president chen shui-bian was awarded the international liberal federation freedom prize. unfortunately, he was not granted a visa from any eu country either, so that he could receive it himself, as his wife did, here in the house in november. nevertheless, we would like to express our dissatisfaction with the failure of the member states to respect their obligations to respect the fundamental right of the president of taiwan to travel freely. the olympic games will be held in china in 2008, the decision has been made and will not be changed, but let us take this opportunity to demand that china use this opportunity to demonstrate that it will abide by all international human rights standards and conventions. recently, china has adopted a practice of separating children born in mainland china from their parents in hong kong. it is a very serious circumvention of human rights and we therefore call on the chinese government to put an end to this practice immediately and to begin family reunification so that hong kong parents can be with their children. i would like to thank mr graça moura again for a thorough and well-considered report which aims at a more effective eu policy on the china question.
