commission. – mr president, as my colleague mrs wallström explained during the debate yesterday, the commission is unable to accept those amendments of parliament which would extend the scope of the proposed directive to include a total and immediate ban on octabromodiphenyl ether – in short octabdes and decabdes. this concerns amendments nos 1, 2, 3, 7, 8, 9 and 10. the reason for the position of the commission is that the risk assessment for these substances is not yet finalised. furthermore, the commission is also unable to accept the amendment that would introduce a future total ban on decabdes from 1 january 2006, should the risk assessments conclude that the substance causes concern. this is amendment no 4. the reason we take this stand is that the ban should be introduced much earlier than 2006, as the risk assessment will be concluded during 2002.
the commission is not - mr president, i would like to point out an error in the english version of amendment no 3, where the last word 'status' should have been 'place'. i therefore ask that this be corrected in the original english text.
- mr president, i would like to begin by congratulating mr fatuzzo on his report on a subject which i know is close to his heart. i have two points, and they are in fact the same two points as those raised by mr kuckelkorn, on the one hand on the open coordination of pensions and on the other on the internal market for supplementary pension schemes. the open coordination, where member states set common objectives and guidelines for future pension policy, analyse developments and exchange experience, is in many ways a good cooperation. it is particularly good that the debate on the future of pensions is no longer just about the national economy and how expensive the next decade will be for the many pensioners. it is good that the member states are talking together about how we can ensure that in the future pensioners can afford good and safe conditions. but i must nevertheless point out the problem with open coordination, namely the democratic deficit. the targets and guidelines agreed by the member states for future pensions are first drawn up by so-called 'high-level' representatives, who are officials sent by the member states' governments, and the agreements are then ratified by the member states' ministers, and it must be assumed that these ministers have a popular mandate. we must assume, because there is no public debate on this. when we adopt legislation in the eu, there is a public debate in the member states. a debate in the national parliaments. a system of consultation of interest groups so that politicians have a factual basis for decision-making. here in the european parliament we have our open debates so that the public can hear and understand the arguments behind our decisions and so that interest groups can have a say in the debate. this basis for decisions and open debate is totally absent when countries cooperate through open coordination. if we are serious about this cooperation and really want it to be reflected in the policies pursued, then the doors must be slammed open when decisions are taken on objectives and guidelines. the national parliaments must be involved much more actively, and local authorities and interest groups must be involved. the european parliament can also play a positive role in this cooperation, because here we bring all cultures together and look at things from a holistic point of view. it cannot be stressed enough that efforts must be made to democratize open coordination and i would strongly urge the council to take up this matter. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the second point i would like to mention briefly is an appeal to the council and the commission to speed up the adoption of the supplementary pension directive. of course, it must be the right thing to do. we must avoid restrictive rules that impede existing systems. but with that in mind, it is important to get things moving, with common rules we can make life easier for employees in companies working across borders and promote competition and efficiency in pension providers.
- mr president, we all know that asbestos is one of the greatest threats to the health of workers in the european union. it is estimated, as a previous speaker has said, that up to half a million deaths over the next 35 years will be directly linked to the serious diseases caused by asbestos. the history of asbestos and the discussion we have had about asbestos, in my view, shows how incredibly important it is that we take the precautionary principle seriously. for many years asbestos was used without concern for its possible harmful effects, and that is precisely why the problem of asbestos will haunt us for many years to come. the proposal put forward by the commission will ensure better protection of workers who are at risk of exposure to asbestos during their work, and it is a very good proposal on the whole. the proposal reduces the limit value for the number of asbestos particles to which workers may be exposed. but given the dangers of asbestos, it is good that the rapporteur is trying to get the limit value lowered further. it is essential that the employer be obliged to provide an appropriate training programme for all those at risk of exposure to asbestos at work. workers must be aware of how to protect themselves and others. the commission's proposal calls for the establishment of common guidelines for the content of the training programme, which should be as precise and comprehensive as possible. i would like to call on the member states today to go further and to set up genuine, formalised training courses in the field of asbestos. the 1999 directive prohibits chrysotile, the last type of asbestos that is allowed to be used. the total ban on all use and placing on the market of asbestos is to be implemented in all member states from january 2005. but from what i have learned, it seems uncertain whether the ban also applies to exports of asbestos to countries outside the eu. if that is true, then that problem should be addressed. it would be completely unethical for the eu to export asbestos for use in third countries. it would be contrary to all the principles on which we are normally founded. i do not know how we are to do this legally, i will deal with it politically and i would like to take this opportunity to ask the commissioner to explain to the commission today how he interprets this problem. is it uncertain whether the 1999 directive actually prohibits exports to countries outside the eu? it would be interesting to have an answer to that question. i would like to conclude by thanking the rapporteur for the enormous work he has done to put this report together. i support it fully.
- mr president, i should also like to congratulate mrs damião on her report. i think it is good that the commission is now tightening up the measures to protect workers from the dangers of exposure to asbestos, which is a particularly dangerous substance, and i can therefore fully support all the additional tightening ups contained in the amendments. as mrs thorning-schmidt pointed out, there is still an unsolved problem with the legislation on asbestos. this was emphasised at the meeting of the council (employment and social affairs) on 7 march 2002. the ban, which will be introduced in the eu from 2005, does not apply to production for third countries. i know that several countries, including denmark, germany, sweden and finland, wanted the ban to be included in the directive, but unfortunately only a minority at the council (employment and social affairs) agreed with this position. i also know that the commission has been reluctant to include a direct ban in the directive itself on the grounds that it would not follow the normal procedure and therefore could possibly give rise to legal problems. at least that's what i've heard. if that is the case, i would urge the commission to find another solution so that we can also ban the production of asbestos for sale in third countries. when special precautions are required of our own workers, the eu cannot of course justify exporting to third countries which do not have the same resources to take their own precautions. it is, quite frankly, immoral and i simply cannot understand the member states who cannot see it. the eu should of course stop such production. in addition, workers involved in production for third countries will be exposed to asbestos, whether the product is sold within the eu or outside the eu. the eu is already notorious for its ambivalent rules vis-à-vis third countries. amending this directive to include a ban on manufacture and export to third countries could be a good start to change this situation.
mr. president, civilization is no longer present in the middle east. i will take commissioner pattens' words to heart: israel is going far beyond the legitimate limits of the fight against terrorism, and the palestinians are doing far too little to prevent the terrible suicide bombings. we must bring civilization back to the area. we must have the rule of law, not the rule of power, so that power is replaced by justice. this is the principle on which european cooperation is based, and we could learn from it in the middle east. the first step must be to allow the un team to investigate the atrocities that have been committed there. it is shocking that the israeli leader has denied the three high-ranking officials access to the area. the former president of finland, the former president of the international red cross and the former high commissioner for refugees are among the victims. israel is not to be given special treatment. israel must accept that all are treated equally and that there is equality before the law. according to the speaker of the knesset, with whom i had the opportunity to speak yesterday, 65% of the israeli population support sharon, but at the same time 80% support the saudi peace process. it's a paradox to me. we see peace agreements between egypt and israel and between jordan and israel. there is therefore a possibility of peace in the region and here in europe we must therefore maintain optimism and hope. what can we do? i believe that we must have human rights representatives present to monitor the respect of human rights by both sides, as we demanded in our resolution. it is again a question of ensuring that the 'rule of law' replaces the 'rule of power'. human rights organizations must be present and monitor the situation. at the same time, we must have an international peacekeeping force in the palestinian territories, whether it is the un, nato, the eu or the usa, but it must be there to bring about peace. there must be a guarantee of an international community, and at the same time israel must not see this as a hostile act. we need a new peace conference, but we also need new participants, new people. as a very respected jew in denmark told me, "there are two idiots in the area at the moment, one is sharon and the other is arafat". we need to get the rule of law back in the area. this is the message of the liberal group, and we friends of israel in europe must be able to criticise the israeli government, and as a dane who is a true and genuine friend of israel, we must be able to criticise without being attacked for being anti-semitic. finally, i would assure commissioner patten that the liberal group will come forward with the money when it is asked for it.
- mr president, i too have just returned from jenin, and when commissioner patten says that 'israeli soldiers have in some cases shown a lack of respect for human life', i must say that i am very surprised that he uses that terminology. there is no doubt that what we saw in jenin was a complete lack of respect for human life. they went in and bulldozed the houses, and to make sure that under no circumstances could they come to anything living or dig up a single body, tanks were driven over them afterward, so that there was almost a highway where the houses had been. if that's not a lack of respect for human life, i don't know what is. there is no way of knowing how many dead people are under the rubble of the jenin city walls. but you can smell the dead. there's a foul smell when you walk around the area, and you can start digging where the smell is worst. if you don't know how many bodies are down there, it's because men and women were separated. the men have not yet been returned to jenin, so it is not known how many are still in israeli captivity, and how many are lying dead beneath these rubble. if we are to condemn suicide bombings, and we certainly must, then we must condemn as strongly as possible what has happened in jenin. we must not just demand that israel withdraw its troops. we must condemn it and we must demand that those responsible be held to account, that israel itself hold the soldiers responsible for these inhuman acts to account. there is no doubt about the number of civilians killed. we have the exact figures from human rights organizations. over 2,000 civilians have been killed. we know how they were killed, whether they were killed by bombing, whether they were shot, where they were shot. we know how many children there are. we know how many men there are, we know how many women there are. we also know that there is a systematic destruction of the entire infrastructure in the palestinian territories. we know that today there is no registration of births or deaths. there are no marriage records. there are no criminal registers, and at the same time it is said that arafat should imprison the criminals, but the registers no longer exist, because they have all been systematically destroyed. i would ask commissioner patten to withdraw his statement on guarantees that this will not happen again. how on earth can we expect - after a completely systematic and very deliberate destruction - to receive guarantees from israel that it will not happen again, and should we really wait or not build the whole structure if we do not receive guarantees from israel. there is no doubt about what israel wants, what sharon wants, but i will not compare the two, because sharon is sharon. he imprisoned last week a leader, marwan barghouti, who has been one of the leading advocates of peace. he's being held in custody, he's had one hour with his lawyer in eight days. he's interrogated 21 hours at a time, only allowed 3 hours of sleep. he is my colleague, he has the same immunity as i do, but he is being tortured in an israeli prison at the moment. it is part of the whole destruction of palestinian society, its infrastructure and its leaders. let's just say it like it is, let's not make assumptions that are evidence enough.
the committee on women's rights has also adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. - mr president, on behalf of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities i should like to thank the commission for its communication, but above all i should like to thank the rapporteurs for some good reports. parliament has always given high priority to human rights and defended them in every context, and this defence has not diminished since 11 september 2001. we have had great success in our relations with the applicant countries, which are also a kind of third countries, in emphasizing human rights and democratisation through the copenhagen criteria and the whole body of eu legislation, and in this context also focusing on human rights and women's rights in general. this applies not only to the applicant countries but also to the developing countries. i do not think the commission's communication is entirely clear on how we are actually going to guarantee the human rights of women. there is no doubt that women in a large number of the countries with which we cooperate are oppressed in a number of different areas. they are very often victims of religious or cultural traditions, and i think we have to do away with the concept of religious and cultural traditions which are used to oppress a very specific group, namely girls and women in general. in this context, i think we should try to involve women more actively and perhaps we should start by developing some programmes with the help of the women who have come to europe, because we do not always have precise policies for how we deal with their situation in our own countries. if we used this approach, we might also gain some experience in relation to the developing countries. i would like to refer in particular to paragraphs 2 and 3 of the opinion of the committee on women's rights, in which we propose that the commission should work very concretely on some aspects of this area.
- mr president, the european union is the civil and economic power which can make a contribution to democracy and human rights. a task that weapons cannot do, namely crisis prevention. the real fight against oppression, for democracy and against terrorism begins here. the eu can pursue an active policy of human rights and democracy through its association and trade agreements. we know that human rights violations have terrible consequences, and when they do, when terrorism occurs, we are all too often left with the question 'who is behind?' and all too seldom with the question 'why?' it's not enough to know "who", we also need to know "why". we know that behind terror lies frustration, oppression, lack of democratic opportunities, lack of human rights. as we see in the middle east today. as we see when individual acts of terror strike random civilians in israel. we have problems in the eu with our policies - not with the fine words, but with our own actions. we accept clear violations of the human rights clauses which are in the association agreement with israel. a clean break with no consequences. we have to stop the hypocrisy. our long debates will lead nowhere unless we are willing to either stop the speeches or let the very fine words be followed by action. the eu is not powerless, we can actually make a difference. we could have made a difference, so that what some of us experienced in jenin yesterday had not become a reality. we are not powerless, let us use the influence and power we have.
- mr president, we are dealing with two very different reports today, but the reality of both reports is that unless incredible changes are made - both in relation to the developing countries and to the situation of women in the western countries, including the eu - then we can forget about equality. but the methods are of course completely different, and i would like to thank the two rapporteurs precisely because the reports show that different measures are needed. i would like to stress in particular, with regard to the developing countries, paragraphs 10, 11, 12 and 14 of the report, which point out, among other things, the need to look more closely at religious, cultural and socioeconomic traditions and the role they play in relation to women in developing countries. it is fine to say 'mainstreaming', but we must not forget concrete action programmes. as far as the eu is concerned, i think that an article in the guardian today has a very apt headline: 'women can have it all - with a little bit of help'. the background to the article is that research in the united states shows that 42% of high-income women are childless. they simply start too late and are under the illusion that women can have it all. they can't. it's something we've been putting in their heads. they can't have a career and a family life with kids. that's why the next headline is also: "a female-friendly state can make a significant investment in the future". i think we have to say that we have an illusion of equality in a number of western countries, but it is and will remain an illusion, and we should not imagine that we will have equality until men also start to claim that they want everything. it is the demands of men that must be put on the table now.
the commission is not - madam commissioner, i know you as a very skilled and hardworking budgetary commissioner. today we are going to talk about something else, namely employment policy. just yesterday i received a call from a danish municipality with a message: 'can you not work to ensure that the member states learn from the eu?' "can you not work to make them realise that it is through local action in partnership between social authorities and private enterprise that the best way to combat unemployment, especially the hard part - the longterm unemployed and the weak, is to work?" in fact, when i received that invitation, i was genuinely happy. i was pleased to see that parliament has done a good job with this directive on eu employment policy. we did the right thing. we have called for more openness in employment policy, for more active involvement of the social partners and local authorities. we wanted to give the individual citizen better opportunities to know and understand this policy, because it is ultimately the citizens, the voters, who have to assess whether their national government is doing enough in employment policy. we have wanted to promote employment policy at local and regional level, through more information on local employment plans, through exchanges of experience on good practice and through requiring countries to explain in their national action plans how local authorities can be involved in employment policy. and it has now succeeded in reaching a compromise which meets parliament's demands on these important points. i would like to thank my colleagues in this house for the unity and the persistence we have shown in this process, for example by putting forward proposals which parliament has put forward in another context, so that there was a good preparation. i would also like to thank the council and the commission for their efforts and their receptivity, which have now ensured that we are getting a good result. we in parliament have attached great importance to the coherence between the employment strategy and the community's action through the social fund programmes. we therefore welcome the declaration annexed by the commission to the agreement on incentive measures. in the declaration the commission promises to ensure this coherence and to keep parliament and the council fully informed of the priorities chosen under article 6 of the european social fund, and in particular of the local activities which will receive quite substantial amounts over the next few years. we will remember that promise. i should also like to say a few words about the employment budget. parliament had proposed a higher amount than the commission's proposal of eur 55 million, and we wanted the commission to do more, so it was natural to ask for more money. but now we have assurances from the commission that the increased effort to provide information can be done within the 55 million euro limit, and we are relying on that. the budgetary framework has not been fully used so far, so it seems realistic. part of the budget is to be used for evaluating european employment policy. we have wanted this evaluation to also look at the methodology used. the open method of coordination is gaining ground in many areas in recent years, and it would be strange if there were not both good and bad experiences from employment policy which could inspire the work of open coordination in other areas; employment policy has now been running for about five years and in that time a lot of experience has been gained. on the whole, i think we have a good result, and it is up to the commission and the member states to complete the new framework, but most importantly, this legislation has given citizens, the social partners and local and regional authorities a tool to develop mutual inspiration and competition to secure many more footholds on the labour market. they have been given a tool to help them work towards the other important objectives of eu employment policy: reducing the rate of exclusion from the labour market, ensuring that we are flexible and competitive, and ensuring that it becomes possible to better reconcile work and family life. i hope and wish that this challenge will be taken seriously by all parties.
- mr president, a big thank you to the rapporteur for a very good job. i would like to say on behalf of the pse group that we are very pleased with the agreement reached. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. unfortunately, unemployment is too high in europe, but we do have evidence that a coordinated employment policy creates far more jobs than when each country pursues its own policy without taking account of what is happening in neighbouring countries. the incentives now agreed are precisely intended to support and strengthen cooperation on employment in the eu. this must be done through analyses, statistics, reports, information activities, evaluations and other means. i think there are a lot of good results in the settlement. it is important that employment policy is not something that is done only behind the desk and in more or less closed meetings of the council of ministers. the population must know what method we are using, even when it is called something as difficult as the 'open method of coordination', and it must be informed about what is happening in the field of employment policy. what is even more important is that the local authorities, who are supposed to be the ones to get things started, are also involved. it is therefore extremely positive that the local dimension has been given even greater prominence in employment policy. i am glad that the commission and the council have come to the conclusion that it is the local and regional authorities that are important in the employment effort. the same applies to the social partners and voluntary organizations. partnerships are the way forward in this area. the practical work of employing people is done locally and therefore local actors must be involved in employment policy. this requires targeted support for local action. one of the ways of making employment policy more effective is to spread good practice in employment policy to all those who are actually involved in putting people into work. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is therefore also important that we have a thorough evaluation of the results and methods of employment policy. i am therefore very pleased that agreement has been reached to increase the budget, albeit modestly, to eur 55 million instead of the initial amount of eur 50 million. i think this is a positive step, precisely because we have reached a stage where we have to demonstrate to the citizens that employment policy really works and that the open method of coordination has something concrete to offer. i would like to conclude by thanking all the rapporteurs, especially for the good cooperation, and wish the commission good luck in putting this into practice.
the commission is not mr. president, this is a fine evening. i think that all those who are concerned with ensuring a better working environment for european workers have reason to be satisfied. the council and parliament have managed to agree on a ground-breaking new directive. this is the first time, as far as i know, that any really binding rules have been laid down on the vibrations or shocks to which workers are exposed during their work. that is why, of course, there have also been strong exchanges of views along the way. we have had a lively discussion about whether the scientific basis was right. we have heard from many, many lobbyists, and not all of them were in favour of the drafting of these binding rules. however, with this directive we have achieved recognition that vibration is a health risk, both hand-arm vibration and whole-body vibration. this is good news, for exposure to these shocks at work can have harmful effects on health. it is also good that everyone, the commission, the council and parliament, have agreed on a method of limiting vibration and other physical agents. the method is to introduce both action values and limit values which should never be exceeded. in view of the fact that we are dealing with a completely new area of health and safety legislation, i think that the agreement reached is very good, and i would like to say this directly to herman schmid and the gue/ngl group, because it strikes a fair balance between the interests of the industry and the protection of workers. it is a very big gain in itself that we are now actually getting a directive. we did not always know that we would have this, but now all european countries have an obligation to take the problem of vibration very seriously. i have therefore accepted, on behalf of the socialist group, that the limit value for whole-body vibrations is at the level proposed by the council in the common position. the directive has been in force for some time, and we must see whether a future revision will enable us to bring the limit down further. there has been a lot of focus on whole-body vibrations, but i would like to point out that parliament actually got the action value down from 0.6 m/s2 to 0.5 m/s2. i think that's a very important victory, because the value of the stock is a very central quantity. why is it? it is because it actually imposes an obligation on employers to take the problem of vibration seriously and to actively prevent workers from being exposed to high vibration. when action values are exceeded, employers must take concrete action - that is why they are called action values - by, for example, changing the organisation of work or investing in better equipment. this is precisely the approach which, on the one hand, provides flexibility and, on the other, places a great deal of emphasis on prevention rather than on actual injunctions and prohibitions. it is this preventive action which i consider to be the most important that employers should now undertake. i also think that the settlement creates a good framework for the sensible implementation of the provisions of the directive. the reports to be implemented at the same time as the directive will also include examples of good practice. in this way, good experiences in one member state can help other countries to learn from them. the same principle we have just been talking about in employment policy can perhaps also be applied in the field of working conditions. of course, transitional periods are necessary to ensure that industry has time to adapt to these new rules, but the council common position included some transitional periods which were a little too long and this has also been rectified. however, for agriculture and forestry, where special conditions apply, there is still a transitional period of up to 12 years. it is a long time, but we should be able to ensure that these professions can also adapt to the new rules. i must also make a few critical remarks. it is difficult, mr president, for ordinary people who do not deal with the european union on a daily basis to understand that it takes so long to complete a directive. when people hear that the first proposal for physical agents dates back to 1992, the reactions are very critical. is it true that it will take us more than 10 years to negotiate a directive? it doesn't promote people's faith in the decision-making process. i therefore call on the commission once again to submit proposals for directives for the two physical agents which are still missing, namely electromagnetic radiation and optical radiation. and now for something more positive. i thank the council and the commission for the very good cooperation, also at the very informal level, and i thank the rapporteurs from the other parties. it has been very constructive and we have actually ended up with a result that we can all be proud of.
- mr president, commissioner, first of all i should like to thank mr heinz kindermann for a serious and thorough report. the common fleet development programmes are the principal problem of the common fisheries policy, since the european fleet is far too large. commissioner fischler said last week that the fleet was 40% too large. this means that at the moment we are, on the one hand, giving money for scrapping aid in an attempt to reduce the size of the fleet, while on the other hand we are giving aid for modernisation, and that is to say that this modernisation and this aid in recent years has led to the fact that the total fleet, the total fishing capacity, has actually been increased. it's a vicious circle that we have to break. that is why we should probably consider introducing a period of non-assistance for the modernisation of the fleet in the context of the reform. i look forward to the commission's proposals in this very area, based on the principle that there are too few fish for too big a fleet; now all member states must live up to the joint decisions and we should actually also give a premium to those countries which have so far complied with the joint decisions in the cap programmes.
- mr president, denmark is outside the eu asylum system because in june 1992 we used the democratic right of the people to reject the eu's decision on the asylum rules of the countries. it might stimulate the debate if i explained the three main lines of the danish 'no'. a few in this chamber, represented by mr camre of the uen group, said no out of a paranoid fear that eu rules would lead to our small country being flooded with asylum seekers. others in this chamber, represented by mrs frahm of the gue/ngl group, said no for the opposite reasons, namely to ensure the best possible protection for asylum seekers. what makes this 'no' fade today is the regrettable fact that xenophobia has now become the policy of the danish government. but fortunately there is a third way, a democratic 'no' that does not change with the political situation. this longstanding 'no' is based on the view that problems which affect local conditions deeply should be solved in close cooperation with local communities and not imposed on us by the more knowledgeable eu institutions. the eu's coercive centralism and rule hysteria are simply helping to create the poisonous breeding ground for demagogues like mr le pen, mr haider and mr camre. not that i am ignorant of the social and economic conditions of this right-wing turn and xenophobia, but please note that the centralized enforcement of rules - without regard to the attitude of the local community - is downright deadly and feeds right-wing extremism.
- madam president, i too, as a participant in monterrey, would like to say that it was clear that it is now clear to many how important it is that we in the rich countries contribute to the fight against poverty in the developing countries and help to ensure economic progress in these countries and to eliminate poverty. we must do this for our peace of mind, and we must do this for the peace of the world. it must be a broad-based effort. the developing countries must have access to our markets so that they can create the basis for their own economic progress. the commission has also proposed that the european union should provide a financial contribution to the countries of central and eastern europe in the form of loans. it is shameful that over the past decade the rich countries have reduced the share of their income they give to the poor countries. i would therefore like to commend commissioner nielson for having succeeded in bringing about an agreement for the eu countries to increase their aid. this agreement requires the most persistent countries to contribute at the level of the eu average today, but with a higher level of ambition. the objective must be at least 0.7% of gdp, which is below the level of most eu countries today and which is unfortunately even lower than that of the usa and japan. experience has shown that there is not always a direct correlation between the amount of aid and the results in terms of development. it is all the more gratifying that with monterrey there now seems to be a shared understanding that development must be achieved through the interaction of donor countries, ngos and developing countries, so that developing countries are given responsibility for their own development and so that, as a condition for aid, they ensure democracy, the rule of law and human rights and combat the corruption that destroys many opportunities for progress.
in writing. - (da) we voted in favour of the report because we believe that the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has proposed significant improvements to the proposal for a directive. we would like to make it clear, however, that the problems of asylum policy must be solved as close as possible to the citizens and not on the basis of centralised community legislation.

- mr president, what is progress and what prospects from the council's report are we talking about? if one were to approach the question with no preparation other than a genuinely humanitarian spirit, one might naively imagine that political action should be directed towards preventing and preventing the persecution and political repression that are the preconditions of the asylum problem, and towards distributing both wealth and political influence, in short, democratizing the wealth of this world, which today is concentrated in a few dominant centres, including the eu, as oases in a world of social, economic and political misery. but that is not the council's view. on the contrary, because the building up of the eu's political and economic power - which necessarily takes place at the expense of the outside world - is always, has always been and will always be the overriding political guiding principle of eu policy. the prospect and progress that the council is talking about is not about providing the best possible response to asylum seekers and immigrants. the interests of foreign immigrants, including those of our fellow men in need, are not our primary concern. no, progress and perspective are of a different, diminished importance. it is about making immigration policy as beneficial as possible for us. it is a question of limiting the right of asylum as much as possible, as the following quote says: 'damming up the flows of refugees'. it is about using our repressive systems to advance against what is called illegal immigration. it is done with a very well-developed legal imagination and with measures, surveillance, fingerprints, the eurodac regulation, the free exchange of personal data, which borders on police state methods. in short, it is a matter of building and fortifying fort europe. it is a matter of course that all these measures are described as progress. we also welcome with a sense of humour these strangers to an area which we call the area of freedom, security and justice. as a danish citizen, i am ashamed to admit that my country is the frontrunner in the overall short-sighted, stunted, xenophobic immigration policy. the danish policy of effectively preventing asylum seekers from exercising their right to asylum has not only spread to the other member states. in addition, the danish parliament is currently considering a draft law containing impressive restrictions, including an asylum procedure that can best be described as 'administrative harassment'. it is being carried out, of course, in cooperation with the xenophobic right-wing danish people's party. i must admit that the prospects for further so-called progress under the danish presidency are frightening.
- mr president, i would also like to concentrate on the relationship between the european union and what mr patten calls the 'super-duper power' - the united states. i have no doubt that the united states is aiming at total global military dominance and probably already has it. the question is then what role the eu should play in relation to the united states. whether we're going to be a "clap tail hanger", as they say in danish. if we're gonna be a bunch of crackpots who back up whenever the u.s. wants us, we have to do it. the other option is that we have to be a competitive number two, and that's what we will be if we throw ourselves into an arms race or an attempt to match the military capability of the united states. the third option is that we choose a very different role, namely to be the civilian, global power that can intervene in crises before they occur and before weapons are needed. what we see is that the united states, first and foremost, has the ability and the need to resolve conflicts through military force. they are doing so because, in my view, they are quite incapable of pursuing a proper foreign policy, that is, a sustainable foreign policy capable of preventing crises and preventing war. the eu, on the other hand, has shown its ability to engage in multilateral contacts and thus also has the capacity to lay the foundations for a more sustainable foreign policy. this is the best prerequisite for a civilian security policy and we are therefore in the best position to become the civilian global factor. the elles report is not encouraging reading and i understand that the commission gave it a mixed reception, but i do not understand that all the possibilities of the eu are being thrown away in favour of the first option, namely to make the eu a tailpiece to the united states.

council. - the danish government has put forward several proposals seeking to tighten danish immigration laws. one of the points of this programme proposes to raise the age limit for family reunification by marriage to 24 years. those are the two questions. i'll answer at two. i would draw the attention of honourable members to the fact that it is not for the council to assess the conformity of member states' legislation with community legislation. this is something which, in any case, falls to the commission, which, as you know, has the prerogative of being the guardian of the treaties.
the council has already taken a number of decisions on the basis of the commission's proposals. the commission is the one who has to decide whether a country's domestic law is in conformity with the acquis communautaire. i believe that if each country or each presidency were to judge the others, we would be entering into a spiral of recriminations here which would be extremely harmful to the good coexistence within the union. that is why we have the european commission, which is an institution with supranational powers, which we have given it, which has the obligation to enforce the community acquis in the countries and which must act as guardian of the treaties. it is therefore up to the european union to be the accusing finger and to tell each country whether its internal legislation conforms to the acquis communautaire. so let me say that i expect the commission to do its job and that the spanish presidency, as i expect from all the presidencies that follow, will be extremely cautious and respectful of the other member states of the union.

- mr president, many citizens are very nervous about the fact that the european union is a kind of slide, a slide that children use and that means that once you have started you cannot really stop. the citizens are afraid that there are 'someone down in the eu', as they say, who are trying to usurp more powers, more power without asking them first. these myths are daily fed by the organised opponents, some of whom we have here in parliament. i am therefore very pleased with this report because it helps to dispel many of these myths. it is here that it is established once and for all that the member states have the competence unless it is handed over to the eu, and it is established that the eu is a dynamic community where competence is shared when it is most appropriate. the report also challenges a catalogue of competences which would make cooperation too rigid. the report is very realistic and it is no longer possible to accuse this parliament of being a fanatical extremist. i hope that the organised opponents in my own member state, denmark, will take this into account. i'll talk to mr. bonde, even though he's not here tonight. a few comments on the individual parts: the subsidiarity principle - i am pleased to see that the subsidiarity principle is not only a legal principle but also a political principle, because we need to focus more on subsidiarity as a political principle and thus force the commission to explain itself better to the citizens. i myself have proposed that a single commissioner be responsible for subsidiarity, because this will give us elected representatives in this parliament the opportunity to hold the commission accountable for the issue of subsidiarity and thus force it to explain itself directly to the citizens and to legitimise its actions to the citizens.

- madam president, first of all i would like to thank commissioner nielson and thank him for two excellent reports. ten years ago in rio a clear sustainable development agenda was set, but nevertheless, since then, progress has been largely in the opposite direction. poverty is spreading, and the gap between rich and poor is widening every day. the use of natural resources is increasing every day, man-made climate change is unabated, and every day species of animals and plants are disappearing forever. there is a real need for us in the european union to take on a global responsibility at the world conference in johannesburg. a global agreement should be drawn up with a concrete work programme for sustainable development over the next several years with some precise targets and some precise deadlines. let me mention a few of the crucial elements: we should take the offensive against poverty and hunger in order to fulfil the commitments to the un on development aid, including by giving the developing countries real opportunities in world trade. we should decouple economic growth from increased environmental impact, in particular through increased use of renewable energy and cleaner technology. we should ensure that we take sustainability into account in all policy areas, whether we are talking about legislation, trade agreements, investment or development aid. we must ensure better and more secure means of implementing international environmental and social agreements. finally, i think it is important that we set more concrete objectives for the use of natural resources and the safeguarding of natural diversity. it can be said that with the lack of preparation for the world conference so far and with a bush administration in the united states there is every reason to fear a failure in johannesburg. we have a huge task on the european side to ensure a positive and binding result in johannesburg.
in writing. - (sv) we do not share the views expressed in this report. the draft report, in paragraph 30, states that 'the exercise by the union of its competences, whether exclusive, shared, complementary or coordinating, must no longer be sterilized by paralysing procedures (lack of initiative, unanimous decision, ratification by the member states).' this means that the eu must be able to take the majority of initiatives in all areas in which it feels obliged to invest, without the member states being able to curb these initiatives or seek the opinion of their respective parliaments or the european people. despite the fine phrases that have been heard about the need to bring the union closer to its citizens, their views will not be asked when the eu is drawing up policy plans. the report would like to see employment policy included in the european constitution. this would ultimately mean centralising labour market policy and adapting it to a unilateral economic policy. we do not think that this is the right way to achieve the union's goal of full employment. to be truly effective, employment policy must be able to be adapted to local needs. the open coordination process which is being recommended today in the field of employment allows for adaptation and anchoring with local actors and therefore needs to be strengthened rather than suppressed and disappeared from community policy, as the report proposes.

- mr president, this is a matter we return to regularly. it's the same alliance every time, and it doesn't make it any better. it seems beyond doubt that the south koreans are providing illegal state aid in relation to wto rules. on the other hand, there is considerable disagreement about what should be done. the commission has sole responsibility for initiating wto proceedings against south korea. but despite the unequivocal calls from the council and the european parliament, the commission, with commissioner lamy as the person responsible for this area, has so far shown an impressive ability to ignore these calls. for reasons that are not entirely clear, mr lamy refuses to open a wto case unless state aid is introduced at the same time for european shipyards. in other words, mr lamy will only try to make the south korean practice illegal if europe itself introduces an illegal practice. i think that in europe we will only damage our own cause by such a course, but i hope that you, mr liikanen, can explain the wisdom of this position, although i doubt it. as rapporteur last autumn i worked very hard against this linking and i continue to do so. state aid has never been beneficial and is costly for the member states. i would urge commissioner lamy, as i did in a letter with my finnish colleague, mrs kauppi, which has not yet been answered, to open a wto case against south korea immediately. the same is proposed by the eldr group in amendment no 3. the danish shipyards have already begun to fire employees on the grounds that they can no longer compete with the massive state aid of the koreans. that is why this is a very important matter, not least for the area in which i have been elected, and i strongly urge that a wto case be brought. on the question of unemployment, i refer to the eldr group's amendment no 4.
- madam president, as the previous speaker pointed out, the time is over when running a business was all about making more money. modern businesses must take other considerations into account, such as considerations for people and the environment. this is of course the view of the pse group, but fortunately it is also the view of modern companies and indeed of modern investors. it is quite clear in this report, and i think it is regrettable that the ppe-de group will not accept that the tool in this context is a mixture of carrot and stick. today companies must present financial statements, but in the future, of course, these financial statements will also have to be combined with annual reports which tell the public what companies have done to live up to their social and environmental responsibilities. the annual social reports must of course give a credible picture of what has happened in this area, and this means that they must of course be subject to independent control, just as the financial accounts. so a few words about the social labelling scheme. i hope that parliament will support the social labelling scheme today and that the commission will also look favourably on this idea. the social labelling scheme will enable consumers to choose products from companies that are socially responsible. it is clear that a number of common standards for the social labelling system should be adopted, otherwise it will only create confusion and consumers will not be able to make their mark and choose the products they want. the eco-label introduced by the european union could serve as a source of inspiration. i also believe, of course, that the social labelling system has the quality of appealing to a very wide range of political groups, because it is a voluntary system, an incentive structure that uses carrot instead of whip.
- madam president, there is nothing new in the fact that companies are taking on social responsibility. it has long been a part of the daily life of european companies to assume a responsibility for employees, the environment and the environment that goes beyond what laws and regulations require. in denmark we have been working for a number of years on corporate social responsibility, particularly with a view to keeping people in work and bringing excluded groups into the labour market. this has been achieved through a dialogue and interaction between businesses and authorities which has been very fruitful. another example i would like to mention is the cooperation between thirteen major scandinavian groups which, together with the world wildlife fund, are working to develop a programme for sustainable development. the key words of this effort are to want the sublime - not just what the law requires. to do it from the heart. to start with the company. the key words are commitment and volunteering. that is why it is also very disturbing to me when many people in this parliament want to regulate voluntary action and force companies to report and explain their actions. i've fought against that. i think it is a good idea, however, to encourage the exchange of experience and ideas in this field and thus support the efforts already being made by companies and organizations to structure and standardise this work. i believe that the dublin institute is well suited to this purpose, with the expertise and network that the foundation already has, and therefore the commission should be very grateful for having raised the debate. the eldr group can support such active efforts to develop corporate social responsibility.
- mr president, all members are entitled to equal treatment, whether they belong to a large political group or sit here as the sole representative of a group of voters. our task is to ensure equality between the elected representatives and that we all have the same opportunities to work in parliament. the political groups are a practical measure designed to enable members to work together with people who are more or less of the same opinion. a political group cannot have special rights to which members outside the groups do not have access. then we wouldn't be equal before the law. the same principle applies throughout the european union, including in this parliament. i am glad that it was expressly stated by the court of justice in the recitals of the judgment where the technical group was dissolved because it expressly stated that it would not be a political group. the judgment requires parliament to examine the various arrangements for members and to ensure that there is no discrimination against members who are not members of a group. the judgment also says indirectly that members who are discriminated against are welcome to bring a new action, so the court will have to ensure that the principle of equality is fully respected. the corbett report is not a response to the court's judgment. it contains a number of proposals to strengthen the groups and it will also make it more difficult to form new political groups. in future, a group must have at least 16 members from one fifth of the member states. that is the compromise that the big groups pushed through. i doubt both the wisdom and the legality of the restriction which could lead to the regionalists remaining without a group of their own and the greens remaining without a group after the next elections. we can agree or disagree with the regionalists, but they have a common programme and a common supranational party alliance through the european free alliance. they are not enough to form a group of their own, but should they not have the right to act as an independent political entity if they so wish? the greens were also a political entity before they were enough to form a political group. should we lose a green group in parliament because an election in germany might again send them under an undemocratic 5% threshold or because they do not have sufficient support in an enlarged eu? or take the italian radicals, who are registered with their international radical party at the un and have a belgian representative on their list. can the 7 italian radicals be deprived of their political equality with other groups? i believe that we have a duty to ensure that all those who cannot form an independent political group can have their proportional rights recognised in the rules of procedure and by practical and pragmatic measures which ensure a real equality. if one political group has the right to put proposals on the agenda each time, this right could be given to smaller groups in turn. if a political group has the right to put forward an infinite number of proposals, then at least once in a while members outside the groups should be allowed to put forward a proposal, have it considered and put to the vote. if a political group is entitled to a certain staff and budget, a proportionate share should be guaranteed for members outside the groups. a democracy is defined by the way it treats its minorities. we're not being fair to our minorities today. we should give our minorities the same right as we who have been fortunate enough to have enough members to form political groups. why not give members outside the groups the right to form a technical group so that they can manage the allocation of the rights of a group among themselves? we're missing corbett 2, which gives each member their full rights.
- mr president, mrs frassoni has asked what we are going to do here today, what problem we are trying to solve, why give medicine when there is no disease. i have to disagree with you on that, mrs frassoni. there is a disease in this house. and it is the debate that has been too predictable and therefore also rather boring for too long. members come in in shifts and read out their two-minute speeches, and the result is a parliament where there is really no real lively debate. we are not giving the people the opportunity to see a parliament where opinions are really being broken down. and that is a pity, because we have a parliament where we have a very lively and spontaneous debate in the committees. but the public just doesn't get to see it. the corbett report is an attempt to create a basis for a more lively debate, and it is clear that there is a very negative objection to this catch-the-eye procedure. but one thing is certain, and that is that if we do not try to do something, nothing will happen. so i think we have to stop this tendency to just go on as usual. it is also excellent that we now have the opportunity to distinguish between what is important or controversial and what is not controversial. this too will make parliament more lively for the people. i should like to conclude by saying a few words on the amendments tabled by the committee on employment and social affairs. as you know, the treaty of amsterdam introduced a new procedure, the open method of coordination, which started with employment policy. what we proposed in the committee on employment and social affairs was simply that parliament's role should be defined in this method. it would create more transparency, it would create more focus on this procedure. and it would also create more democratic control within the open method of coordination, and so it is very regrettable that this knowledge or this insight has not reached the committee on constitutional affairs and that it has not been appreciated that it was important also in this area, which is quite new and very interesting for the union, to try to make democratic progress. i thank mr corbett for an excellent report.
- mr president, i was actually preparing to give a positive speech on the improvement of the quality of blood products at european level, but i have to say that mr nistico's action as rapporteur on this matter is subject to criticism. as late as last week we reached an agreement with mr nisticò in the presence of the commission and the council, and this agreement led us to table amendment no 19 as a compromise, which moves the definition of blood donation free of charge into the recitals. it was a compromise, even if mr nistico is shaking his head. it's just disgraceful what's going on here. what mr nisticò is doing is subject to criticism, and i would therefore say that we must be prepared for the fact that we will be asking for a postponement of the vote later today. i do not want to be part of this process. that is not the way to treat colleagues in this parliament. both the commission and the council, whose presidency was represented at the meeting, accepted this compromise proposal, as did mr nisticò on behalf of his group and as rapporteur. it is therefore absolutely incredible that you come here to the second reading of this parliament and announce that you will not accept the agreement we have reached. this is at least the last agreement i have made with mr nisticò, and as i said we will do everything we can to postpone the vote on this matter so that we can discuss this with the leadership of the ppe-de group and with the ppe-de group coordinator in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy. what has happened in this case is simply too wrong, and we will not accept that.

- madam president, this report is a technical part of the long-standing efforts of the eurocrats to build a political, parliamentary space in the european union. the starting point was the maastricht treaty, which persuaded the outside world and the electorate to believe, without success in denmark, that the building of european political parties was an important factor in integration. the creation of a european political identity will be achieved through these manoeuvres aimed at building a political parliamentary space. this political architecture is backwards. it is like a builder who starts his building with the top floor and builds from the bottom instead of starting at the foundation. these efforts are doomed to failure. they are followed by a whole series of measures concerning european political parties, but politics is not formed on the basis of preconceived notions of a european space, where the top floor probably belongs to the regions of our lord. the closest historical example of this kind of backward political architecture is the tower of babel, and as we know, the result was not good, and the construction was never completed.
the council. - madam president, the johannesburg summit is a further step in the process begun in rio de janeiro to achieve sustainable development worldwide. in rio, the so-called agenda 21 was defined, which contains the fundamental objectives achieved during this century. however, despite significant progress, the global environment has continued to deteriorate significantly. all states and all stakeholders, ngos, the private sector, etc. will meet in johannesburg to devise new mechanisms to achieve this objective. the summit is intended to tackle together the economic challenges - development defined as pure economic growth -, social - poverty reduction, achieving the goals of the united nations millennium declaration - and environmental challenges themselves. it is understood that these three challenges are interrelated. the outcome of the johannesburg summit is expected to include the following elements: a political declaration marking the renewed commitment of world leaders to achieve the objectives of the millennium declaration and the shortcomings in the implementation of agenda 21, an action programme with key action commitments, voluntary cooperation initiatives involving all actors, governments, civil society and the private sector, in a series of initiatives to implement the action programme. the european union is actively working on the six priorities for johannesburg in the areas of health, water, energy, trade, global public goods and sustainable patterns of production and consumption. i would like to begin by referring to the preparatory work carried out during the spanish presidency. two preparatory global conferences were held in new york during this period: prepcon 2 and prepcon 3, and the last preparatory conference, prepcon 4, which took place in bali on 24 may and includes the ministerial meeting from 4 to 7 june, began on 24 may. in the meantime, the community positions have been prepared in brussels in a process whose stages are: the conclusions of the environment council of 4 march which were endorsed by the barcelona european council, the conclusions approved by the development council of 30 may and, finally, the conclusions of the general affairs council of 17 june, based on the preceding ones and on the results of prepcon 4, which will be endorsed at the seville european council as the european union's overall position on johannesburg. at the level of the 15, the discussions focused on the following issues, which will be resolved at the general affairs council on 17 june, just before seville: firstly, the coherence of community policies, without prejudice to the adaptation of community policies to the internal and external objectives of sustainable development. the johannesburg summit must not become a conditioning instrument for the reform of community policies. secondly, the abolition of subsidies which are harmful to the environment. although in chapter 2 on trade a compromise formula was reached, reducing, if necessary eliminating, subsidies considered harmful to the environment, there is still no consensus among all member states on the compulsory or automatic nature of the elimination of such subsidies. the council will also refer to the swedish initiative of the ombudsman to facilitate exports from developing countries to the community market. it needs further study before it can be accepted and included in the johannesburg summit as a community initiative. the commission has therefore undertaken to submit a document in the future on the possible launch of this initiative. financial issues, such as those relating to the possible link between financing and future initiatives to be developed during the johannesburg summit, will also be addressed, and these financial issues are those which are indicated for the additional oda resources committed during the monterrey conference to be used or allocated to initiatives to be developed in johannesburg. mobilization of alternative sources of financing, aiming to link alternative sources, such as the so-called tobin tax, with global public goods and the possible extension of the initiative "highly indebted poor countries". secondly, i would like to refer to the prospects for the european council in seville. the presidency has devoted a great deal of effort to preparing the johannesburg summit. the european union must make a very important contribution to this summit and show great leadership, because we are the global standard-bearers on sustainable development. i think that the union's ratification of the kyoto protocol is a good sign, and also because the international preparations for the summit clearly need european leadership. at the moment there is disunity at international level and a lack of clarity in the objectives to be achieved with the summit. prepcon 3 was disappointing, the new proposal for an action programme must be more concerted and concrete. the united states wants to minimize political issues as much as possible, it would even like to abolish the political declaration and the programme of action in order to concentrate on implementation. the us approach to implementation is also novel: it wants to create new 'transfunds' in which public and private money can flow together, which is rejected by several european partners. the presidency believes that the european union should play a role similar to that which it played in monterrey: seek consensus with our main partners. we must coordinate our efforts as soon as possible with juscanz, which is the group of the usa, japan and canada, and with the group of 77. maximalism and political confrontation lead to nothing. monterrey was a success because the european union worked at all times for consensus, and if practical results were achieved in favour of the developing countries, we must try to do the same in johannesburg. if we fail to do so, the spectre of durban and the problems that were there during that summit will weigh on johannesburg. 1,500 non-governmental organizations are going to participate, some of them very radical. some seem more interested in confrontation than in achieving practical results for the benefit of the developing countries. at prepcon 4 in bali, we tried to reach consensus on a short action programme with a clear focus. a political statement must be very short, very clear and avoid confrontation, and it must address implementation and practical issues. it is important that the european union should have a communication strategy for the johannesburg summit. we must have a clear vision of what we want in johannesburg and how we want to convey that vision to world public opinion, but above all to the public opinion of europe, which, as i said before, has been leading on all these issues. finally, the presidency believes that the global pact to be created on the basis of the results of monterrey, doha and johannesburg must be based on the principles of democracy, respect for human rights and good governance. the financial effort of the developed countries must be matched by the fight against corruption and the firm implementation of democratic values in the developing countries.
commission. – mr president, the european commission's role in the un ministerial preparatory conference in bali, at which i represented the european commission, was facilitated by the comprehensive reports prepared by mr papayannakis and mr lannoye and discussed with my colleague, mr nielson, at parliament's part-session last month. the commission agreed with parliament last month that the preparatory process for the world summit is not going as well as we would like. this remains our assessment after the bali meeting. as we have heard, three documents are being prepared for johannesburg: an action plan on sustainable development, drawn up under the leadership of the un-appointed chairman, dr salim, secondly a political declaration and thirdly criteria for the launching and monitoring of partnerships to implement the actions and political commitments agreed in johannesburg. none of these documents was finalised in bali, but while the bali meeting to prepare for johannesburg was not a breakthrough, it was not a breakdown of the process either. there was intensive eu coordination throughout the meeting in bali. ministers agreed that the key eu objectives to be achieved were, firstly, the setting of clear targets and time-frames, especially for water, sanitation and energy, secondly the establishment of a clear linkage between the political commitments and targets in the proposed action plan, and their implementation through initiatives or partnerships, and thirdly an agreement on the shift to sustainable patterns of consumption and production. some progress was made in these areas in bali, as well as on issues such as health, africa, small island states and the environment and some of the differences between delegations were reduced. however, the meeting was dominated by the developing countries' insistence on trade and finance issues. the g 77 group pressed for the creation of a world solidarity or poverty fund based on voluntary contributions from public and private funds. whilst it said that it did not wish to reopen agreements reached in launching the trade round in doha or at the conference on financing for development in monterey, the g 77 group was seeking signals from the developed countries which could lead to increased market access for developing in addition to least-developed countries and ways of constraining those that have not yet clearly committed increased resources under the monterrey consensus to do so. since the developed countries have always made it clear that market access questions would have to be discussed in the world trade organisation in the context of the doha development agenda and that there would be no new public funds for development beyond what had been pledged in monterrey, the positions taken by the g77 representatives, led by venezuela, inevitably led to an unfortunate standoff between the developing and developed countries. this blockage in the means of implementation meant that we were unable to achieve the breakthrough we were looking for in bali. whilst useful progress was made in cleaning up the text of the action plan, large parts – including some key eu objectives – have not been agreed. all these issues will have to be sorted out either in informal meetings or actually at the johannesburg summit. a successful outcome in johannesburg now depends on finding ways of bridging the gap between the developed and developing countries over trade and finance issues. the eu position has a lot of appeal for developing countries, not least because it seeks, inter alia, to promote common commitments on increasing trade-related assistance and capacity building beyond pre-doha levels, to search for innovative ways to encourage trade in sustainable products and to encourage the emulation of the european "everything but arms" initiative. the european union therefore can, and must, play a constructive role in bringing the parties together. however, unless a satisfactory solution is found, there is a risk that the johannesburg meeting will be dominated by acrimony over the issues of trade and finance and the focus on sustainable development risks being lost. if that happens, the opportunity to make progress with sustainable development could be missed. now is the time for leadership. the south africans are now in the driving seat and will need our help and support to pull things together in the coming weeks. the european union will need to play its part in overcoming differences in the next few weeks. future meetings, such as the seville european council and the g8 meeting, as well as bilateral contacts, will provide important opportunities for review and discussion by developed countries on how to respond to g77 concerns without reopening the doha and monterrey agreements. we will need to work closely with g77 to re-establish a climate of trust and partnership and explain our globalisation and sustainable development agenda. in addition to the political context, we will also continue our technical work to build support for our partnership initiatives, which can bring real benefits to developing countries in terms of poverty reduction, health and educational improvements and environmental protection. together we must ensure that the european union plays a leading role in ensuring that the outcome of johannesburg is ambitious and action-orientated. the european parliament is an advocate of sustainable development. we must bring a sense of urgency to the preparations for johannesburg in the short time that lies before us.
- madam president, too many people in the world are hungry, and too many people are living in poverty. too many people are afflicted with deadly diseases, such as malaria and aids. it threatens the growth and development that we all want. the way forward is more growth and prosperity in developing countries - growth and prosperity that must be coupled with sustainability. there are two factors that are crucial to growth and prosperity. it is the possibility of free trade across national borders, free trade, and it is uncorrupted, limited government in the form of a rule of law. it is the customs walls, not free trade, that create poverty. happiness creates both poverty and stagnation, and that is why we must secure an agreement that breaks down customs barriers, ensures free trade and free access to the market for developing countries, and we must start with ourselves. but we must also demand something from the developing countries. we must demand and strengthen the demands for democracy, respect for human rights and good governance. we must secure international agreements on environmental protection and the control of deadly diseases. the developing countries are aware that their prospects for prosperity are destroyed if they are isolated. unfortunately, rich countries often impede the growth of poor countries by imposing tariffs, by setting up trade barriers under the guise of environmental regulations and by supporting their own agricultural production. rich countries thus bear a large share of the responsibility for the problems of developing countries. a fundamental change of course is needed. we need to tear down all customs barriers and eliminate all trade barriers. our challenge and task is to get all the rich countries to meet the un target of 0.7% of gdp in development aid, but we still have to demand economic reforms and better governance from the developing countries. in johannesburg we must take a decisive step towards a global reform programme for freedom and prosperity. we owe it to the future and this is what parliament and the union must work for.

promise they made in rio. the developing countries therefore have a legitimate expectation that these issues will be addressed. the developing countries want and should have the assurance that the
