- mr president, i should like to begin by thanking mrs hautala for the very great work that has been done on this report and also the commission for having actively contributed to its success. and i am not just saying this out of politeness, but in real recognition that there are people who have actually been able to make a difference in this area and have demonstrated in practice that it is possible to set a good agenda as an individual member. i think that with this report and the work that has been done, we have passed a milestone in the history of equality in the european union. first of all, we have been given a definition of sexual harassment, and this is very useful. there are a number of countries which have not really addressed this issue before. we have also prohibited discrimination against women who are pregnant or in labour and, in some cases, against men on maternity leave, if there is national legislation to that effect. and finally, we have the opportunity to take positive special measures where one sex needs it. all in all, i think it's very good. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of women are respected in the member states. at a recent meeting with the applicant countries, they were very concerned about this very issue because they lack data and because they need to have a means of pressure on their own governments to obtain this data. but i also think we have to say that we are going to be watching this in our own countries because it is something that the member states can easily get away with. i have unfortunately experienced this myself in my own country, but i will not dwell on it any further. however, i think it is up to all of us to keep an eye on this, both by helping the applicant countries and by taking on our own responsibilities in our own countries.
- mr president, i can support the separation of foreign and security policy from the rest of european cooperation and the limitation of the composition of the council, but i must reject any attempt to give the larger countries a larger share of presidencies and posts. all countries must be equal in the eu, before, during and after the seville summit. the proposed transparency reform does not open up the work of the council of ministers to the public, let alone to the elected representatives of the european parliament and the national parliaments. the commission proposal is to be discussed openly the first time it is presented, and it is right that this openness should extend to all proposals. however, voting will be opened later in the process, but since in practice only a small number of proposals are voted on, this part of the exercise will be purely cosmetic. we do not know any more than we do today, when the council publishes about 50 votes a year. the problem is that 70% of the decisions are finalised in the council working groups, 15% by the ambassadors in coreper, only 15% of the proposals are submitted to the council, and we have no figures for what percentage the ministers actually see. the requirement here must be that at least all elected representatives have full access to the legislative process. the commission has given us an assurance that we can have members' contributions to the council working parties. i am glad that yesterday, in the conference of presidents, the spanish foreign minister, mr piquet, promised us full access to the council working parties during the co-decision procedure; that is a good start. why not decide that any eu proposal must be submitted to national parliaments and be treated in the same way as national legislative proposals? a purely national bill can always be changed - a european union bill can never be changed. it is for this very reason that it should not be treated less thoroughly than proposals which allow for the rapid correction of errors or unintended consequences. it could be assumed that the luxembourg compromise veto would continue to apply, perhaps in the form proposed by georges berthoin, monet's right-hand man, whereby the prime minister would be obliged to defend a veto at the next summit. it could also be required that the national parliament decide to use the veto in an open resolution. it must not be easy to block, but it must be possible if the european union is to be a cooperation between the parliamentary democracies of free countries; that is our wish, in the europe of democracies and diversity.
the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities has adopted a report on the situation in the european union. - mr president, on behalf of the committee on women's rights, i would like to thank commissioner verheugen for his work on equal opportunities. every time the committee on women has spoken to the commissioner, he has not hesitated to say that the acquis communautaire, if i may say so, should also apply in this area. the fact that it is only implemented at the last minute in a large number of countries is not the fault of the commissioner. however, it does mean that i would like to encourage the commissioner and his staff to continue to monitor the area so that we are not only getting the necessary facts at all times but also making sure that the countries are supplying them. and i would like to say to the president-in-office that i think the member states have a special obligation to women, because it is one thing to get the governments to accept membership, but it is another to get the people to accept it; women make up more than half the population, and what they have been able to register so far in the applicant countries is a high level of unemployment, which has particularly affected women. it is very important that attention be paid in particular to the women's field when we come to the next stage: convincing the populations that enlargement is indeed a good idea, and here i believe that the member states can contribute and that the member states and the commission together can make a special effort.

- mr president, the two proposals for regulations and reports which we are considering today are important supplements to the directive on the release of gmos which we adopted last year. rules on traceability and labelling are of course absolutely necessary, partly to ensure effective quality control, but also to enable products to be withdrawn from the market in the event of unforeseen adverse effects on our health or the environment, and finally to give consumers a real choice between gmo products and non-gmo products. i therefore fully support the line taken by the committee on the environment - it will ensure that we have an effective traceability system and that we as consumers have a free choice. we get information on whether a product consists of or contains genetically modified organisms and we also get information on whether a product is made from genetically modified organisms. of course, this also applies to animal products such as milk, eggs and meat derived from animals fed gmo products. we have a right to information, as consumers, both on the contents and the production methods of the food we consume. i can also support the limit for unintentional contamination with genetically modified organisms being set as low as possible and at a maximum of 0.5%. i would also like to make a comment on the multinational chemical companies behind the gmo products and, incidentally, also on the british government, which has the strange position that there should be no labelling, except that companies should be allowed to label their products as gmo-free. that would be a disastrous solution. firstly, the scheme will probably not be used very much by companies - it would be voluntary. for reasons of liability alone, not many companies will use it. but the second is that consumers are left completely in the lurch by such a system. all products that are not labelled gmo-free can be either gmo products or non-gmo products. in this case, consumers will not be informed, and this is of course the intention of the chemical industry. finally, tracing gmos will be impossible if there is no positive perception of gmos at all stages of production and distribution. there is no way of going back to gmos if they prove to have negative effects on our health or the environment. in the light of history, it is undeniably surprising that the british government is in favour of a system which prevents traceability in the food sector. the marketing strategy of the gmo industry is something i simply do not understand. the argument is always for as little information as possible for consumers. do you really think that this is the way to build confidence in genetically modified food? it is completely incomprehensible, and therefore i can only say that i understand the vast majority of consumers in europe. the fact that consumers are constantly told that they should know as little as possible means that they do not trust these products. - mr president, in 1994 there were 179 countries, all eu countries included, that adopted the icpd programme on universal access to reproductive health in 2015. one might have expected that progress would have been made in this area since 1994, but unfortunately the opposite is the case: the arab countries have entered into an unholy alliance with president bush to prevent the work of the un in achieving the icpd objectives. i have just been to the us congress in washington for a hearing on bush's mexico city policy. i was with a member of the russian duma who was able to report that after the ippf provided reproductive health facilities in russia, the abortion rate has been reduced by as much as 25%. why would you do that? the answer is quite logical. if you have an unwanted pregnancy and go to an abortion clinic instead of having an abortion yourself, you will always be informed about other options than abortion, you will be informed about how to avoid unwanted pregnancies in the future and you will be warned about the possible risk of hiv infection. the policies which this report proposes the member states to adopt will not lead to more but to fewer abortions. that is why the campaign against the report by those who oppose abortion is absurd and in some cases downright distasteful. i am thinking of the email sent by the truth of god, which claims that racial hygiene is actually being introduced to get rid of the poor, and that the ippf has been set up by the british eugenics society. such a request is an insult to our intelligence, but it should also be followed up by a defamation suit. a vote for this report is a vote to ensure the fundamental right of all people to make free and informed decisions in their emotional, sexual and reproductive lives and to ensure that everyone has the same opportunities to develop and protect their sexual and reproductive rights - not least to be protected from hiv infection. the desire to guarantee basic rights should be enough to vote in favour of the report. the risk of hiv transmission is such that it is essential to vote in favour of this report.

- mr president, there are 800 million undernourished people in the world today. it is about the same number as when the first food summit was held in 1996, so let's face it: the effort to fight world hunger has failed. in this context, as many colleagues have been saying, it is shameful to see the indifference of western leaders in their absence from the latest food summit in rome last month. the world's hunger problems are largely due to the hypocritical policies of the european union and others. the policy which is supposed to be for the poorest people in the world is too much governed by the eu's own narrow foreign and trade policy interests. officially, the eu and the other western organizations speak in favour of free trade and liberalization as the solution to the world's problems. but liberalisation really only covers developing countries, where the demand for liberalisation completely destroys their ability to build an independent, sustainable production. earlier this year, oxfam published a report concluding that the eu is the world's most protectionist market to developing countries. it's time we put our house in order and get to the root of the problem. we have a lot of work ahead of us if we are to succeed in our quest to eradicate hunger. we could start at least by abolishing the eu's protectionist agricultural aid schemes, suspending our fisheries agreements with third countries which are helping to destroy the livelihoods of the world's poorest, establishing a development policy which is genuinely aimed at combating poverty and is not guided by the fight against immigration, terrorism and the concern for one's own jobs, and - last but not least - supporting the right of the poor countries to self-production and access to water, land and biodiversity.
the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have to say that i am very pleased with the way the commission has been working. i would like to begin by thanking you, mrs mckenna, for your excellent report and the european parliament for its continued support for our efforts in the negotiations on the seafo agreement. our efforts have paid off, for we are on the verge of the community's ratification of this convention. since joining the negotiations on this convention in 1997, the community has been actively involved in it and will now be one of the first contracting parties to ratify the convention. we are therefore making a decisive contribution to its rapid entry into force. the road to this convention has been exemplary in terms of positive and active multilateral cooperation in international fisheries. this convention implements the latest developments in the law of the sea and is a real demonstration of the commitment of all parties to responsible fishing in a sea region whose stocks are very sensitive. i would like to emphasize three points: firstly, the convention establishes a good system of monitoring and control of fishing activities in this region. secondly, it provides a good balance in the distribution of fishing opportunities between coastal states and developing countries. and thirdly, the convention has adopted the dispute settlement mechanism of the international law of the sea. this will give seafo the opportunity to become a powerful organisation for managing resources responsibly, for fighting against illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing, and for benefiting a number of coastal states whose economies are heavily dependent on these resources. i now come to your amendment, mrs mckenna, which the commission can only accept. it is a fact that the un convention entered into force without the community's ratification. the 1998 council decision provided for rapid, simultaneous ratification by the community and the member states. nevertheless, the internal ratification procedures are still pending in three member states. this prevents the community from becoming a party to the un convention. i have personally called on the council and the member states on several occasions to speed up this process. i can only support your further requests that we take account of the sensitivity of stocks, especially deep-sea species, as you have mentioned earlier, in the scope of seafo, and that the community should be more committed to the widespread application of the precautionary approach in the management of these stocks by seafo. i can assure you, madam president, that the community will continue to play an active part in the work of seafo. in our communication on the reform of the common fisheries policy, we made it clear that we want all coastal states to work together to ensure sustainable and responsible deep-sea fishing.
the commission is not - mr president, mr commissioner, the commission has recently presented the proposal for the reform of the future fisheries policy, and in this light the commission's report on monitoring the implementation of the common fisheries policy, which my report deals with, is a very important tool. we need to use all the lessons learned from the previous fisheries policy so that the future fisheries policy can lead to some clear improvements. improvements must be made because there are too many gaps in the existing fisheries policy. one of the clearest examples is the msf programmes, which have not enabled effective control of the number of fishing vessels, have not ensured the necessary reduction of the fleet, and have not ensured effective control of the real capacity of the fleet. certain member states have failed to comply with their obligation to inform the commission of the fishing effort of their vessels or have done so only occasionally during the period covered by the commission's report. unfortunately, there are very wide differences in the way in which the member states meet the conditions and thus also the requirements for reporting catches to the commission. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the fisheries sector is not left behind. this also applies to the reporting of data, the implementation of joint decisions and the use of sufficient resources to ensure full implementation of the cfp. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the protection of the environment are respected. it is also necessary for other reasons, in particular to win the support of fishermen and their respect for the policies that are decided. the fishermen must also be assured that these policies are implemented in the same way in all member states. the responsibility for monitoring the application of community law in accordance with community principles naturally lies with the member states. there is a strong indication that the member states need a little more help in meeting this responsibility, and the community ultimately has the overall responsibility for ensuring that implementation is satisfactory. member states have established legal frameworks for controls and have designated competent authorities in accordance with the traditions of their respective judicial and administrative systems. member states have also granted fisheries inspectors judicial control powers and the power to initiate sanctioning procedures. the commission's report, which is a very good report, is a good report, and i think that the commission should be aware of the fact that the commission is not in a position to take any action to prevent the fishermen from being caught by the community. in the commission's report i also complained that fisheries inspectors are not sufficiently trained. i can read between the lines that there are fisheries inspectors who can't even tell a herring from a brisling. if i am right, if that is the case, then there is certainly something to be done - not least in view of the fact that the training that the inspectors receive is partly financed by the european union. so it is simply unacceptable that the commission has been able to find that there is a glaring lack of training and experience in how to carry out the basic control of compliance with the common rules in force. it is high time we got the member states to give priority to their obligations. it is a question of making the allocation of responsibilities more effective at national level, so that monitoring, inspection and control are not shared by seven authorities, as is the case in one country, for example, which means that no one has the real responsibility. the follow-up of infringements shall be effective and adequate resources shall be allocated to the proper purpose of monitoring and inspection. i have proposed to the commission that a system of rewards, for example in the form of additional quotas, be devised for those member states which comply with community legislation and, of course, that some more severe sanctions be introduced for those countries which do not comply. i am quite convinced, mr commissioner, that the key to success in our fisheries reform lies in the area of control.

it is here that experience is gained, knowledge is acquired and it is here that one has a concrete sense of what works when the objective of increasing employment is to be achieved. it is therefore essential to draw on local experience. i would say to the commission that i am glad that the social fund has included local action in the way it has. the commission has promised us a continuous explanation of how it will use the appropriations at local and regional level in article 6 of the european social fund, which has been allocated 40 million euro this year and next year. we will keep that promise to the commission. it is important for parliament to follow developments in this highly priority area. i also look forward to the open coordination involving the local and regional level and to the new national action plans and the joint employment report telling us what is happening at the local and regional level. i would add to this the question of more active information efforts by the commission in relation to local and regional authorities. the aim of open coordination is not just a few experts, officials and top politicians meeting and confirming each other that we have a good policy. no, the objective of open coordination of employment policy should be to give the elected representatives a better basis for decision-making and to make the electorate more aware of the opportunities we have in the field of employment policy.
- mr president, madam commissioner, first of all i would like to thank mrs gillig for the great and very competent work she has done on this report. it has become a well-balanced report, which i support. there are parts of the report where there are some basic positions with which i cannot agree. globalisation and free trade are sometimes presented as something which is detrimental to social development in the world, and i believe that fundamentally the opposite is true. for developing countries in particular, free trade is the way out of poverty and social hardship. social development and workers' rights are best developed in countries with open economies, while the worst examples of violations of basic workers' rights are found in countries that are closed off from the outside world. i therefore believe that free trade is fundamentally beneficial for social development, but having said that, we must of course ensure that the ilo conventions are effectively respected so that workers are guaranteed decent working conditions, because i agree with mrs gillig that free trade and economic growth are not enough. the commission's report shows that the task of ensuring compliance with the conventions is one which the ilo has not been able to carry out, and the question is what can we do. i would reject trade sanctions as an instrument by its very nature, but it is important that the eu puts the issue on the agenda in international forums. unfortunately, not all countries share our view on workers' rights. so we have to push. it is also true that workers' rights are becoming a natural part of the effort to combat poverty, so that when we give foreign aid we also take workers' rights into account. finally, it is important that we make an effort in the field of education, that we incorporate knowledge of workers' rights into vocational training so that they can be implemented, both in our part of the world and in the poor countries.
in writing. - (da) we would like to make it clear at the outset that we are in no way opposed to the fight against racism and xenophobia which is the subject of this report. we have, however, chosen not to support the proposal because we are deeply convinced that legal and domestic matters are best handled by the member states themselves. we are very doubtful about the legal basis of this framework decision, the very specific provisions of which have the character of a supranational decision.
in writing. - (da) i have two main reasons for voting against the proposal. action against the callousness that manifests itself in racism and xenophobia must not be translated into criminal sanctions, as the text proposes. criminalising is at best unnecessary, at worst harmful and leads to a series of deleterious effects, including a reduction in freedom of expression. 2. the a 'common criminal access in the eu' as proposed in the text is not allowed by title vi of the teu. in reality, the regulation is of such a scale that the decision cannot be translated into the form of a 'framework decision'. thus, 'racism and xenophobia' are not among the types of offences that can be regulated under article 31 (e) - 'organised crime, terrorism and drug trafficking'. the framework decision is therefore contrary to the treaty and constitutes a clear breach of the danish ria exemption agreement.
- mr president, according to the russian constitution, journalist gregory pasko would have committed criminal offence if he had withheld information on environmental issues or disasters that could endanger human life. that is what articles 41 and 42 of the russian constitution say - those are excellent articles. however, he has not done so. on the contrary, he has consistently reported on how the russian pacific fleet dumps nuclear waste and old weapons in the pacific ocean, thus creating a threatening environmental catastrophe. for doing so, he has been found guilty of high treason by a russian military court and sentenced to four years in prison. everything was done behind closed doors, and seventy journalists were expelled from the courtroom. last month, his conviction was upheld at the military division of the russian supreme court in moscow. it is deeply tragic for gregory pasko and it is deeply tragic for the democratic and human rights situation in russia. this shows that freedom of expression and the principles on which our union is founded are very much in short supply in russia. we must make clear in this parliament our rejection of laws and judgments which restrict the freedom of the press, and we must call on the council to express our serious concern at the forthcoming eu-russia meetings. the union is giving russia financial support and in this context the commission must pay close attention to ensuring that the projects we support respect our demands for freedom of expression and the independence of the media and the judiciary.
commission. – mr president, i am going to reply on behalf of mr patten: it is a fortunate coincidence that i was the commissioner who had the opportunity to propose this legislation two years ago in parliament. now, finally, we have the adoption of the anti-discrimination legislation at european level. it was the first time that we agreed at european level that there should be no kind of discrimination in employment on the grounds of sexual orientation. this case is not a matter of discrimination; it is a matter of fundamental human rights. the european commission closely followed the case of the 52 men arrested in the so-called queen boat affair and the conduct and the outcome of the case raised several issues of concern. the commission was especially troubled by the use of national security legislation, with no right of appeal for the 23 imprisoned men, and by the inflammatory language used at the trial and in the media, which undermined official egyptian claims that the men were charged primarily for debauchery rather than sexual orientation. egyptian president mubarak has recently ruled that all but two of the 52 men arrested in the queen boat case should not have been tried in the security court and their case will now be reheard in a misdemeanour court. a decision on the referral is expected to be given on 16 july. this is an important and potentially positive development, although its impact is mitigated by the possibility that even those acquitted in the first trial will be retried. the european commission will continue to follow the queen boat case and other similar prosecutions. the european union's concerns have already been communicated to the egyptian authorities through the normal channels and will be reiterated if appropriate. the european union is committed to the defence of human rights and condemns the prosecution of individuals or groups for their sexual orientation. this commitment is integrated into the european union's external policy as a matter of principle and in the form of concrete measures and actions. at a global level the european union supports the protection of human rights by grants to civil society groups and non-governmental organisations through the european initiative for democracy and human rights. at a bilateral level, the association agreements increasingly govern the european union’s relations with third countries and require that such relations "shall be based on respect of democratic principles and fundamental human rights as set out in the universal declaration of human rights". the recently signed european union-egypt association agreement contains such a clause and respect of human rights is explicitly identified in the text as an "essential element" of this agreement. the association agreement with egypt provides an important additional forum for both parties to discuss issues of human rights and democracy.

commission. – mr president, first i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr stevenson, and also the members of the committee on agriculture and rural development for their constructive attitude to this communication. at the outset, we would do well to recall the context in which the present discussions about protein crops arose. since the council decision of 4 december 2000, the use of processed animal protein has been banned in feed for all animals. because the use of animal meal for ruminants had already been forbidden for a number of years, the ban mainly concerned the pig and poultry sectors. shortly afterwards, the european council in nice took note of the commission's intention to analyse the supply of and demand for oilseed and protein-bearing crops in strict compliance with the financial perspective. this analysis resulted in the commission papers presented in march 2001. let me summarise the conclusions which we drew from our analysis of the situation and of possible policy options. firstly, the ban on meat and bonemeal for non-ruminants would create a deficit of 1.5 million tonnes of soyameal equivalent. secondly, increased aid for eu domestic production would produce disappointing results: little extra output at a high cost. furthermore, our degree of self-sufficiency would scarcely change. thirdly, increased aid for oilseeds would bring us back into the blair house agreement's restrictions on areas cultivated with crop-specific aids. since the presentation of our report, we have continued our reflections not only on plant proteins but indeed across the board. this culminated in the mid-term review of the eu's common agricultural policy presented by the commission on 10 july 2002. the commission is of the opinion that public expenditure in the farm sector must be better justified. besides supporting farming incomes, it must yield more in return in terms of food quality, conservation of the environment and animal welfare, the countryside and the farming landscape, and enhancing social balance and equity. for eu consumers and taxpayers, the review should ensure better value for money. we will be discussing these proposals in the autumn. turning specifically to protein crop production, let me remind you that we propose introducing a separate supplementary aid of eur 55.57 per hectare. we also envisage integrating the aid schemes for grain legumes and dried fodder into the list of decoupled aids. the current provisions on non-food crops will be replaced by non-specific aid for energy crops. the mid-term review of the cap gives us a chance in the context of the new round of wto negotiations to oblige our trading partners to accept our demands on, for example, the recognition of registered designations of origin, animal welfare, food safety and the precautionary principle. our new system of income support dependent on production output will avoid distorting trade and should not have a negative impact on the developing countries. unlike the situation during the uruguay round, the european union will be in a position to take an active part in negotiating the agriculture chapter of the wto doha round, fortified by a strong negotiating stance and a level of credibility which the united states has lost with its recent farm bill. finally, let me say something on the feed ban relating to fishmeal. the extended feed ban prohibits the use of fishmeal intended for ruminants, not because of a potential tse risk of fishmeal itself, but for control reasons, as we have discussed here on a number of occasions. in particular it may be difficult to exclude the presence of land animal proteins in fishmeal or feed containing fishmeal. the reallowance of fishmeal in ruminant feed therefore mainly depends on the availability of methods of differentiate between fishmeal and prohibited animal proteins. several researchers are working on this at the moment and i believe there is an opportunity to revisit this particular issue under the comitology procedure that is set out in the animal by-products regulation for which i have responsibility.
- mr president, commissioner, first of all i would like to thank mr stevenson for a very well-worked report. i agree with the conclusions and agree that the correct diagnosis has been made. it is therefore a matter of concern that the eu's self-sufficiency in vegetable protein has fallen to less than 23%. market-related price relationships have made it advantageous to buy imported oilcakes, especially soya-bean, but climate risks and changes in production in the countries we source from make the eu far too vulnerable. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. i would also point out to the commission that the measures taken are actually weakening the european cereals market by opening up the possibility of importing cheap cereals from russia and that they are only contributing to making crop production less economically viable; at the same time, lower cereals prices in europe are increasing pig production, which in turn requires greater imports of soya beans. this initiative is therefore not conducive to european plant breeding, nor to european production of proteins. i would also like to emphasize that the ban on fishmeal, which the commissioner has been advocating, is not professionally justified. it is not technically correct to argue that we have to keep fishmeal out of the protein supply because of the fear of a mixture of meat and bone meal from ruminants with fishmeal. we must say to those countries which are in control of their production, that is to say, those countries which can really keep the production of proteins separate, that they must of course also have an advantage, so that those countries which cannot do so are not the ones who are punishing.

- mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, madam commissioner, commissioners, i would like to thank you for your reply. my group welcomes the discussion on the measures needed at european level to overcome the unprecedented damage caused by the floods in the federal republic of germany, austria, the czech republic and slovakia. i would like to express once again our profound horror and sorrow for the victims and our sympathy for their families. the immense material damage and the destruction of the livelihoods of thousands of people require a large-scale and coordinated response - since the 1992 edinburgh summit, the architects of the european union have cultivated the view that citizenship of the union does not replace national citizenship but, on the contrary, complements and extends it by creating rights of the union. it's a fake version of reality. at the same rate as citizens' rights are defined as union rights rather than rights based on national law, at exactly the same rate the meaning of national citizenship is being eroded. citizenship of the union is simply integration by the back door. this political pickpocketing is at once anti-democratic and repressive. vital parts of the countries' judicial systems, including the criminal justice and police, are proposed to be absorbed by the citizens of the union. it is believed that these genuine values - democracy and the rule of law - can be acquired with the two billion kroner which the commission has just set aside to strengthen the legitimacy of the european institutions. "there is something rotten" - not in denmark, but in the eu. it goes without saying that third country nationals should have the same rights as national citizens, but this can be done without these measures.

- mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would also like to begin by expressing my sincere thanks to mr schmid for his very expert guidance in the preparation of this opinion. thanks to his knowledge and expertise and his determination, we've got a pretty stringent report. i also very much agree with his view that the organization of european employment policy should be more bottom-up than top-down. i agree with him on this, even though we have very different political views. we have all raised the question of whether the european employment policy has worked, and we have indeed acquired a more common language between the countries in some areas. however, unemployment is still high in many countries and in key countries it has almost settled. there is a lack of structural reforms and a weak ability to live up to the employment strategy, and what then? commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to congratulate mr duff, not only as rapporteur of the report but also as a member of the convention which drew up the charter of fundamental rights. the report we are discussing today is based on the previous positions adopted by parliament during the preparation of the charter in the course of the year 2000, and these recommendations fully meet the objective of constitutionalising the charter. i am speaking to you as a commissioner, but i cannot of course ignore the fact that i am also representing the commission at the convention on the future of europe and, in that capacity, chair the working party on the charter. without going into detail, i believe i can inform the house that the members of the working group on the incorporation of the charter into the treaty have a broad consensus on the answers to the technical questions we have discussed, which concern the modalities and consequences of a possible incorporation of the charter into a future constitutional treaty, as well as the modalities and consequences of a possible accession of the european union to the european convention on human rights. i think there is a great deal of agreement between the recommendations in mr duff's report and the conclusions of the working party. i would first of all like to emphasize that i agree that the charter is already a well-established reality in european life, used by citizens, by advocates general, by decisions of the court of first instance, by the european ombudsman, and by national courts, including the highest level, by the constitutional courts of the member states. the duff report also repeats the observation that the integration of the charter will not create new competences for the union. the charter is already clear on this subject, but it could be made more explicit in the horizontal clauses which provide for the incorporation of the charter into the treaty, especially to allay fears expressed by some. the charter itself is not a source of powers, it is not a legal basis for the union to take legislative action, it is a benchmark of values which must be present in all the policies of the european union. similarly, the charter does not limit the competences of the member states in any way. it is not intended to replace the human rights system of the member states, nor is it intended to replace the role of national constitutions. i therefore share the rapporteur's view that the charter must now be given binding legal force and constitutional status, in accordance with the arrangements to be decided upon and which will depend on the future structure of the treaties. two alternative solutions seem to be emerging in this respect. firstly, the one which deserves the support of the majority of the members of the working group, the integration of the charter in a single document, in the form of a title or chapter one of the constitutional treaty. or, alternatively, the insertion in the work of the constitutional treaty of an article referring to the charter, the text of which, in its entirety, is contained in a specific part of the constitutional treaty or in a protocol. it is also perfectly legitimate not to try to amend the provisions of the charter at this stage before it is validated in the constitutional treaty. the charter represents the political commitment of the previous convention, which this convention must respect. this does not, however, preclude certain technical adaptations, particularly as regards the so-called general or horizontal clauses. the charter working party has worked towards this end and the aim is to find adaptations which do not affect the substance of the provisions but are only intended to clarify the meaning of the current horizontal rules. accession to the european convention on human rights has always been viewed positively by the european parliament and the commission. the rapporteur reiterates this point of view, which i welcome. the members of the working party on the charter consider that the union's accession to the european convention is fully compatible with the enshrinement of a charter with constitutional value and binding legal force. and on this subject i believe that the outcome of this working party will also be expressed in a very broad consensus. if such an accession were to take place, the european union would be subject to external control, which would be similar to that exercised by the european court of human rights in strasbourg over the constitutional courts or supreme courts of the member states. in this sense, there is no relationship of subordination between courts and the autonomy of the community legal order is not affected. this question is closely linked to the question of the future legal personality of the union. the task of the convention will be to introduce into the constitutional treaty a constitutional enabling document so that the union can accede to the european convention on human rights. it will then be for the union to negotiate accession with the council of europe and to decide, in accordance with the usual procedures, on the time of accession and the modalities thereof, without in any way calling into question the individual commitments of each member state to the european convention on human rights and its additional protocols. i sincerely hope that the incorporation of the charter into the constitutional treaty and the union's accession to the european convention on human rights will be two fundamental milestones in the process of constitutionalising the european union, in which the values and principles of fundamental rights are recognised as central.
- mr president, i should like to begin by thanking the tireless rapporteur for his work on the charter. it is important that it is discussed vigorously at all times and also discussed how it should be placed in the context of the work of the convention. i would also like to thank commissioner vitorino not only for his support in principle for the continuation of the charter, but also for pointing out that it is not easy to inscribe the whole charter in the future constitution or the basic treaty or wherever it should be placed, but it must be there. what i think is important when we discuss these issues as politicians is that we are constantly in the tension between vision and reality. the vision is clear enough, we can all hope - i shall return to the substance for a moment - that the charter will be given a total position. the charter is important. it contains important human rights and other rights which are important to ensure for european citizens. on the other hand, we must also face up to reality, namely the political reality of today, and ask ourselves how much we can manage when we are 15 countries - or, in fact, 25 countries, i hope, when we start the intergovernmental conference. it's always the tension field we need to be aware of. i'm a supporter of the charter. i always thought it was a good charter. i have also worked on the charter in relation to the danish constitution and i must say that the charter is somewhat more forward-looking and better than, for example, the danish constitution, if one only reads the constitutional text and does not have the whole historical background. i do not think that all existing constitutions are ideal, but on the other hand i also think that the charter is at the moment becoming a bit of a sacred cow. i therefore think it would be good to see whether there are any articles which are more important and more significant than others and whether there are any issues which might be said not to be the most important to include in this report. i would like to thank the commissioner for his openness and for making it clear that it is not even that simple.
- mr president, the report's theme is supposedly the impact of the eu charter of fundamental rights. but the report seeks in vain to deal seriously with this crucial legal and political question. i shall confine myself to two points. firstly, why do we need a european charter when we have the european convention on human rights, to which all the member states of the european union have acceded? the answer is: because the eu as such has not joined the convention. but the eu should of course - by simply amending the treaty - join the convention, and the report recommends that it should do so. but when that happens, clashes between the court of human rights and the court of justice will be inevitable. the report's statement on frictionless cooperation is pure legal nonsense. secondly, the crucial part of the charter agenda is to promote the permanent expansion of the union. the charter is a building block for the union's federal structure. a federal state has both citizenship and a constitution. citizens of the union are subject to the federal authorities of the union, as stated in particular in paragraphs 7, p, x and n. all this involves a crippling and monstrous interference with the national legal order, and the fact that this is shameful is a sufficient reason to vote against the report.
- mr president, there is no doubt that for many countries the easiest solution would be to insert the charter by a reference to article 6 of the eu treaty. it is probably the easiest solution for my own country too. but i still think we need to think bigger and further. if the aim is to enable citizens to identify themselves in the treaty, they must be able to look up the treaty or the constitution and read directly what rights they have in relation to the eu. i therefore believe that the best solution is to give the eu legal personality and to accede to the convention on human rights. the maximum solution would give the court of human rights more authority than the court of justice. but in pursuing the maximum solution, we must also be open to the legitimate concerns of certain member states, particularly when it comes to questions of national identity. i might have missed the fact that the rapporteur had just inserted a single sentence saying that we should be aware of this concern. i think that if we listen to the legitimate concerns of the member states and at the same time seek the maximum solution, we will be able to combine the big vision with reality.
- mr president, three years ago the tampere european council gave the go-ahead for a common european policy on asylum and immigration. the aim was to adopt common rules to make the eu an area of freedom, security and justice. that was the starting point. three years after tampere, we are now faced with a concrete step towards the realisation of this vision. in the eldr group we are pleased that our views have been taken into account in the committee's work. we believe that this is a coherent and well-balanced report which has come to the house. it is important that we have common minimum standards for refugees. and for the rules to be effective, it is also important that all member states have a common definition of what a refugee is. i am pleased that the report contains general rules on whether or not international protection is justified. today, some member states are facing problems related to asylum and immigration policy. every year, large numbers of undocumented asylum seekers enter the eu. they are putting a strain on the whole system and seizing the resources that could have been spent on those who have a real need. we must fight illegal immigration and human trafficking, but we must help genuine refugees. it is this balance that we must strive for and which the report, in my view, makes possible. an important prerequisite for this proposal to work is that we have a common definition of refugees with subsidiary protection status. it shall be based on relevant international conventions, such as the european convention for the protection of human rights and the un convention against torture. i support the idea of subsidiary protection status being part of the common refugee policy. but one is asylum policy, another kind of integration. it is important that third-country nationals are given the opportunity to get on with their lives in their new country. several eu countries already know this. for example, denmark and sweden have adopted an active integration policy. the proposal incorporates issues relating to common rights for third-country nationals legally residing in the eu. they must form the basis of a sound and necessary integration policy. on the whole, the report is balanced in relation to the problems we are facing and i hope that it will be able to gather a broad majority in this parliament.
in writing. - (da) we voted in favour of the lambert report today because it improves in many respects the minimum standards for refugees and persons enjoying subsidiary protection status which are unfortunately being set up under the auspices of the european union. the report seeks to widen the circle of those concerned and to improve the situation and rights of all. for example, the commission proposal concerns only third country nationals and stateless persons and not citizens of the union, which the report tries to correct. the union would send a strange and dangerous signal to the international community if the member states, all of whom have signed the geneva convention, could decide that they are excluded from the scope of that international agreement because they consider that the convention cannot be applied to their own citizens. the commission also envisages extending the rights of persons enjoying refugee status. this is of course a positive thing, but it should also apply to persons enjoying subsidiary protection status, as the rapporteur recommends. the june movement is in favour of a humane refugee policy, we believe that every human being has the right to live in dignity, but we do not think that the best way to safeguard this right is to develop a common european refugee policy based on the first pillar, the supranational pillar, which is the proposal in this report.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats in the european parliament voted today in favour of mrs lambert's report (a5-0333/2002). we agree that the european union must establish minimum standards for the recognition of third country nationals and stateless persons as refugees or as persons in need of international protection for other reasons. however, we would point out that the directive falls under title iv of the ec treaty and is not applicable to denmark, see the protocol on the position of denmark.
- madam president, i should like to touch on a very small part of the budget, namely the budget for the decentralized agencies, the so-called 'agencies'. the problem for both agencies is that they are physically located in countries where inflation is running faster than the eu average, and this is eroding their budget and thus their ability to do the job. this is the case of the dublin agency, which works to develop social dialogue, particularly in the candidate countries, and the turin agency, which develops vocational training in the balkans and under the meda and tacis programmes. the commission's budgeting does not take account of this inflation problem and i believe that several new agencies are currently being set up to ensure the necessary expertise in administration. they are very different in their structure and tasks, and i hope that in future we can have a budgetary treatment which better takes account of the different structures and different needs of the agencies.
- mr president, on very solemn occasions we speak in this parliament and in the european institutions about how we should proceed to bring the union closer to the citizens. one way of doing this would be to give citizens the opportunity to participate, to be participants and co-actors in all the decision-making processes that take place within the eu institutions and between us. the prerequisite for citizens to be able to play this role is that they can get a feel for what is going on before decisions are taken. that is why the principle of access to documents is so important. as my colleague mrs malmström said, we are all aware that there are confidential documents which are difficult to disclose. however, i think that the european parliament has come to a dead end. we've come out of a place we shouldn't have moved into. as guarantors of the citizens, as guardians of democracy, as the institution that is to be the democratic guarantor of this project, we have now moved into an area where we agree that the citizens will not have access to documents unless we ourselves do so or a small group of us does so. in this way we are helping to promote our own interests, or rather the interests of a small group of members of parliament, at the expense of the interests of european citizens and their ability to participate in the democratic game. the whole process of access to documents has been, as my other colleague from the socialist group described it, a process that has taken place behind closed doors. this is a problem in itself, when we are talking about openness, access to documents. that is why i have no warm feelings about this document.
- mr president, the ppe congress in estoril has now voted in favour of full transparency in the legislative process in the council. in this way, all the major political forces support the openness that was put on the agenda when the danes voted no to the maastricht treaty on 2 june 1992. we have seen some progress, but we also have some good things. the commission now publishes its agendas and minutes of its meetings. it is important to know who said what and how they voted. the commissioners must be accountable to the public. the council has distributed the minutes of the working party and the negotiations on transparency. we must have the same access to the working papers and minutes of other working parties. and we need access now so we can follow the legislation before it's passed. we are now discussing a compromise proposal on parliament's access to confidential information, but the new rules will limit the access that all members have under the treaty. i think we could go further by going to the court of justice, as has been done with great success in the case of real agreements. i shall therefore not vote for the compromise. the truth is that the european parliament has never negotiated progress. there have been individual members who have lobbied alongside the ombudsman and active citizens who have won court cases on the fly. it is at the court of justice in luxembourg that we have made the most progress, not in negotiations between the council, the commission and parliament. that's the sad fact. the european parliament is always ready to make demands, but it remains silent when a framework agreement is about to be concluded or a transparency regulation is being negotiated, which now carries the risk that documents which are currently public in denmark and sweden will be regarded as secret documents from the moment they are sent to the eu. i am also not satisfied with the new agreement and would ask the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy to go to the court of justice with the documents we cannot obtain, such as the screening reports on the adaptation of the applicant countries to eu legislation. the screening reports are a very good example of documents which should be available to everyone in this chamber and in the parliaments of the applicant countries. i doubt we can get them under the new rules. i cannot imagine that the court will prevent us from obtaining documents which are clearly part of the legislative process. i think we can make more progress by fighting rather than making a meager compromise now.
mr president, as the commissioner's remarks this evening show, we rarely disagree with the commission on the direction we should all take when we talk about health and safety at work. we are all concerned that too many people are suffering permanent physical harm in the course of their work. what is perhaps worse is that in many areas there has been no real improvement in the working environment in the eu. it is against this background, madam commissioner, that we had hoped that the commission would have been more ambitious in the strategy on health and safety at work and, at any rate, in its follow-up. i had hoped that today we could have more than just schedules for the various activities. as the rapporteurs have pointed out, the situation in the field of health and safety at work is such that since 1992 the resources available to the commission in this area have been drastically reduced. i realize there are different ways of calculating it, but the point is, the money doesn't add up. these are cuts at a time when the problems of working conditions have become more complex and more extensive and when we are faced with new problems of working conditions. we have to work with different tools, we have to look at prevention and we have to help the national authorities. there are many very complex tasks to be solved and the challenges we face in the form of directives cannot be contained in a three-minute speech. we know that problems of working conditions can become a women's problem, as mr evans has also pointed out. we know that small and mediumsized enterprises require special attention. there is an urgent need for a directive on the whole muscle and bone problem. stress and bullying require joint action and we also know that there may be a need for a general recognition of occupational diseases in a minimum directive. in view of all the measures which we all consider necessary, it is perhaps our greatest disappointment that the commission does not intend to follow up this strategy with a real action plan. when the commission says that it wants to be part of the timetables, our answer must be that it should be made even more binding and we must propose that a real action plan be drawn up. if the commission agrees that we must have timetables and that we must make it more binding, then hopefully at the end of this debate, when there will probably be more people demanding an action plan, it will explain to us why it is not taking the full step and going ahead with this strategy and putting forward a real action plan. we know that it will become more binding on the european parliament, the commission and, above all, the member states. i hope we can move on. we need an action plan on health and safety at work.
- mr president, i would like to thank the commission for a good report. i would also like to thank mr hughes for his emphasis on the importance of focusing on the implementation of legislation. danish employers often claim that they have to comply with the occupational health and safety regulations, unlike the situation in many other countries. i am frankly tired of having to listen to it without being able to honestly contradict them. i would like to be able to refute such allegations in the future. it is therefore important that we focus more on the practical implementation of the legislation on health and safety at work, so that a good and safe working environment is actually created in all workplaces in the eu. that said, we must also consider carefully what regulation is best for the protection of the working environment. legislation is not always the right way to go. sometimes the exchange of experience, training and the development of social dialogue are a better way of improving things. a good example is the fight against stress and bullying. stress and bullying are problems that can only be solved by better cooperation in the individual workplace. i therefore also believe that the fight against stress and bullying is a matter for the social partners.
- mr president, a study carried out by the bilbao institute shows that selfemployed workers are more likely to be injured at work than workers, even if they are killed. we also know that there is a very serious under-reporting of accidents in the case of selfemployed workers. for example, a study in the uk shows that only 5% of accidents for the selfemployed are reported, compared with half for employees. it is also true that the selfemployed are more prone to accidents at work than employees because of their limited resources and perhaps because they do not have to comply with these provisions. the commission is therefore to be commended for raising the question. it is clear that this must be stopped. the selfemployed should not be allowed to drive in free wheels when it comes to eu rules on working conditions. but we would have liked the commission to have looked at some of the amendments which try to make the recommendations more binding. i am aware that there is no treaty basis for this. if we all agree that the problem of the non-application of the legislation on health and safety at work to the selfemployed is a problem, is it not time we tried to change the treaties? perhaps it should be a call to the commission to put forward a proposal on this subject in the convention currently in session.

- mr president, it is a matter of great pleasure that ireland has now accepted the treaty of nice with its specific declarations on irish neutrality. this has cleared a serious obstacle to enlargement. the enlargement is a historic event, which both the commission and the danish presidency have prepared in a very competent way, in my opinion. the applicant countries have made an outstanding effort to prepare for accession to the union. i feel that the remaining problems are understandable. it is now up to the 15 heads of government to show leadership and responsibility so that next week the presidency can have the necessary joint mandate to conclude the negotiations with the 10 applicant countries on the basis of the commission's call from january. no new conditions for enlargement are to be set. the agricultural and financial reforms should not be linked directly to enlargement, but we also know that at the copenhagen summit in december the present and future members of the union should be able to agree on at least some principles for a future agricultural and financial reform, which we know will be necessary from 2006. it is also important that the negotiating result be fair to the applicant countries, that a fair competitive situation be created, perhaps with a little shorter transitional arrangements in the field of agricultural aid, so that positive referendums can be carried through in the new member states. i also hope that a worthy solution can be found to the kaliningrad question, so that the small enclave does not become a conflict zone but a positive player in the baltic sea region, with full respect, of course, for lithuania's sovereignty and its wish to be a member of schengen. we must absolutely avoid a corridor solution which resembles previous solutions in europe on similar issues. the transit question should be resolved in another way, possibly by means of a special passport system. but thank you to the danish presidency for its good leadership.
- mr president, this enlargement project is a national prestige project for the small country i come from, denmark. the criteria for the admission of new member states were formulated at the copenhagen summit in 1993 and the process is to be completed at the copenhagen summit in december. it's irrational that this is a prestige project for this small nation, but it does strengthen our self-esteem, and that's always nice. the problem is that there are so many unresolved questions. the belgian maastricht negotiator, who is an economist, has argued that there are a number of financing problems which have not been solved. he said that everyone wanted enlargement, but no one wanted to pay for it. why should the british, for example, still have their rebate scheme, while the much poorer candidate countries have to pay full price? that's the problem. another problem which is completely overlooked and which lies under the very, very spacious carpet under which problems are swept away is the question of the credibility of the progress reports. i do not see anywhere in these analyses a sober attitude to the crucial question of the final and decisive copenhagen criterion, namely that the applicant countries must not only fulfil the acquis communautaire but also have the administrative, legal and political structure necessary for its effective implementation. a swedish report last week points out that these problems are very serious. a simple calculation would show that the legal, administrative staff simply do not have the training. the whole basis for the actual implementation of the acquis communautaire is not there. these problems have not been dealt with, and the question for my danish friend and former colleague, the danish minister for europe, is: what are you going to do about this totally under-reported problem?

the commission. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. the european union has a particular responsibility to ensure sustainable trade in endangered species, as we are one of the largest markets for such species and their products. in addition, we must help developing countries develop effective strategies for the protection of wild species. as you know, in september the commission proposed guidelines for the community in preparation for the conference in santiago. our proposal was based on respect for the scientific criteria which determine which cites annex to which species should be added. these criteria also include the precautionary principle. i am pleased to note that the commission's position differs only slightly from the views expressed by the committee on the environment. the commission accepts the recommendations concerning whales, freshwater turtles, sea turtles and seahorses. i am also pleased that the committee has decided to introduce a recommendation for ratification of the gabarone amendment, which gives the community the opportunity to become a contracting party to cites. the commission supports the inclusion of commercial fisheries in the annexes when they meet the relevant criteria and their management is otherwise not sufficiently regulated at international level. in this context, i would like to join you in supporting the inclusion of the brown and whale shark in appendix ii to cites. the same principle has been used by the commission in its assessment of the proposal to include species of the australian and antarctic toothfish in annex ii. i realise that there are important biological reasons for this proposal. at the same time, i am also aware that the exploitation of these species is currently regulated by the commission for the conservation of antarctic marine living resources (ccamlr), to which the community is a party. although it has only a limited number of members, the catch documentation plan established by ccamlr contains provisions very similar to those contained in cites. i believe that inclusion in the cites list should be supported if it can promote the implementation of this plan and encourage other countries to implement it. however, the commission considers that the proposal in its present form will not lead to the achievement of this objective. i hope that at the conference we can agree on amendments to make the proposal more effective. i can also confirm that the commission is opposed to the resumption of commercial trade in ivory, as long as it has not been clearly demonstrated that such trade will not lead to increased poaching or have a negative impact on the status of elephant populations. the evidence on this issue does not allow conclusions to be drawn so far. therefore, it is appropriate to choose a precautionary approach. however, we must distinguish between the commercial trade in ivory and the question of which of the cites lists elephants should be included. we cannot ignore the fact that there are large numbers of african elephants in several countries in southern africa, where populations are currently listed in annex ii. these populations are well managed and increasing in number. therefore, the proposal of kenya and india to include all african elephant populations in annex i is not justified by the relevant scientific criteria established by cites. rather than reinvigorating the conflict, i believe that the community should seek solutions which promote the conservation of elephants throughout their range and which can be supported by all elephant range states. finally, mr president, i would like to inform the european parliament that in addition to the points you raise in your resolution, a large number of other proposals have been put forward for the conference which will have a positive impact on the conservation of species, and which the commission supports. these include a proposal to include a genuine mahogany in annex ii and proposals to improve cites working methods.
mr president, i do not think that any of us here are contesting whether or not there should be an ivory trade: of course there should not. but that has nothing to do with the fact that southern african elephants should not be moved back into appendix i. i do not know whether you have seen the letter we received this week from the european sustainable use specialist group. it reminds us that such a decision would be directly contrary to the scientific advice given to the parties by iucn, who are the official advisers to the cites conference on species listing proposals. this advice points out that the elephant populations of the southern african countries mentioned do not meet the criteria for appendix i, but meet the criteria for appendix ii. so, scientifically we have absolutely no reason to move them. nor should we if we want to maintain the populations because it is quite clear that the use of wild living resources, if they are sustainable – and they are in southern africa – is an important conservation tool. the social and economic benefits derived from such use provide incentives for people to conserve them. as it is, if we were to take them back into appendix i, it would send the wrong signal to those countries that have a small elephant population. it would say to them that if they try to invest money in preserving their elephants, they will not get back any revenue. so they will pay out for the elephants, instead of paying for schools or hospitals. what kind of incentive to preserve elephants does that give to countries with small elephant populations? it is a pity that more of you were not at the presentation by the minister of wildlife and tourism of botswana. she said that they loved their elephants. they want to preserve them and the way they can best do this is to keep them in appendix ii.

- thank you, mr president. the commission's proposal for the mid-term review of the agricultural policy, madam president-in-office, is a good basis for starting discussions on the future reform of the eu's agricultural policy. the decision of the brussels summit on long-term financing provides the basis for enlargement. that is good, and the presidency should be commended for it. a reform should put farmers and consumers at the centre. it should be dynamic and should be a continuation of the liberalisation that started at the berlin summit in '99. it should include adjustments to market economies and world market prices and thus also a reduction and change in the known policy of aid. the period up to 2006 should therefore be used to develop a reform of the eu's agricultural policy that will make european farmers competitive on the world market of the future.
